APPENDIX F

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

109

REPORT OF THE GRAND LECTURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
My Brethren, one of the required duties of the Grand Lecturer is to
deliver, at each Annual Communication, a report of the services rendered during
the year and to set forth, with respect to ritual, the condition of the lodges.
Before doing so though, I must express my sincere gratitude for your electing
me to this office. You have granted me the opportunity to continue to work for
this Fraternity in the capacity I know best. In my 35 years as a Mason, I have
had the good fortune to be taught by a succession of inspired Grand Lecturers,
starting with Doug Lemons and including Joe Hammond, Ulysses Patropulos
and Hal Macy. By your vote, you have allowed me to try to carry on their work
and build upon the stones which they have so carefully laid.
All year I have heard our Grand Master say that he who speaks least is
appreciated most. I subscribe to that dictum and shall attempt to follow his good
advice.
During this year we have continued the instructional practices which
have proven themselves effective. There were two ritual conferences at which
the Inspectors demonstrated their proficiency in all three degrees. We have
continued to stress that the Officers’ Coaches and not the Inspectors are
responsible for teaching the ritual within the lodges. To that end, the AGLs and
Inspectors have held schools of instruction at which the Coaches were required
to exemplify the work. In addition, the AGLs have conducted Master Mason
seminars which I have attended when possible and hope to attend more often.
Of course, the Inspectors conduct the required schools of instruction in their
individual Districts, at which attendance by the lodge officers is mandatory.
With the assistance of the AGLs and other students of the ritual I
believe that we have finally identified and published a listing of all (or nearly
all) of the errors in the current ritual cipher. Capitalizing upon the very valuable
work of Worshipful Carl Serkland, a Past Master of Mosaic Lodge, the admin
website will soon contain a correct version of the Funeral Ceremony. We
developed guidelines to satisfy Past Grand Master Fred Sorsabal’s decision to
permit the use of costumes in the second section of the Third Degree. Following
the premises to permit early presentation of the 75 year award, which was
adopted at the last Communication, we have prepared a similar resolution to
permit early presentation of the Golden Veteran’s Award.
Once again, there was a Ritual Competition. This year, the focus was
upon an individual lodge, where the work is actually done, rather than on
District or Division teams. Each competing lodge had to demonstrate the Third
Degree opening, Flag presentation and balloting, work done in nearly every
Stated Meeting. I was surprised, therefore, when only 11 lodges elected to

110

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

compete. Local competitions were held to determine District winners. These
competed to find Division finalists and these finalists competed to identify the
State winner. These were not easy tests. Teams were judged on every word and
every minor movement. The results were remarkable. All Division teams
scored in the middle to high 90’s. Every competing team should be applauded
for their diligence and dedication to our ritual.
We are currently in discussion with the Masonic Formation team and
others to try to develop alternative media by which our ritual lessons may be
illustrated and expanded upon. While the Code requires that each degree be
completed on the day it is begun, nothing prohibits the use of an illustrative
DVD for example which could be shown at a later date with an accompanying
discussion of its contents, perhaps by one of the Traditional Observance or
Research lodges.
As to the condition of the lodges, there is much by which we should be
encouraged. We’ve seen a striking increase in the number of men joining our
Fraternity. I’m told we have seen more candidates this year than in any of the
past 19 years. I’m encouraged by the interest in the T.O. or Traditional
Observance lodges which teach our history and philosophy as well as the ritual.
They are gaining many lessons which are lost in the short versions. I’m
encouraged by the Masonic Formation Task Force and the several Lodge
Leadership programs which are helping to turn around lodges in difficulty and
who may otherwise have to seek consolidation as a last resort.
While we are seeing so much that is good, we can’t ignore the
disturbing news. Too many lodges have difficulty in finding men who are
willing to serve as officers. We hear too many reports of disharmony within a
lodge. It seems we have forgotten our goal of spreading brotherly love and
affection, and finding who best can work and best agree. Part of this is due to
very new Masons being forced into offices before they have had a chance to
truly learn our standards of philosophy and leadership. We have masters who
have been Masons for only two or three years. Lastly, there is a very disturbing
element intruding upon our Craft. That is the hazing of candidates. We hear
stories of candidates being subjected to emotional and physical abuse before,
during and after their degrees. Brethren, these practices are, in and of
themselves, unmasonic and criminal and will not be tolerated. I look to you as
leaders of your lodges to take whatever steps are necessary to guarantee that
they never occur within your lodge. The responsibility is yours.
I continue to owe a debt of gratitude to my friends and colleagues, the
Inspectors and the Assistant Grand Lecturers. You carry the burden of
perpetuating our ritual, that which differentiates our Fraternity from all others.
Without your dedication and unceasing efforts, this Grand Lecturer would truly
endeavor in vain. Words are not adequate to express my admiration and
gratitude. I extend my thanks also to Past Grand Lecturer Hal Macy who has
proved time and again that he is always ready to lend his assistance, advice and

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

111

encouragement. I must include a sad note. We are sorry to announce that Jack
Rose, AGL of Division IV, has decided that the time is right to move on and
leave this Committee. Jack is a true phenomenon. He is incredibly productive,
proactive and anticipatory. He sees and takes steps to address many situations
before we are even aware we have a situation. His work on this Committee will
be sorely missed. We needn’t sorrow for his departure however his work on
behalf of our beloved Fraternity will continue in new and exciting venues.
Finally, Most Worshipful Mel, I must thank you and Betty and your
officers for your warm and gracious acceptance of June and me into your Grand
Family. It’s been a great ride and one we will never forget. Also, thanks go to
John Cooper and the entire Grand Lodge office staff who are always ready to
take the extra step in their cooperation and assistance. And to all of you, my
Brethren, mere words fall short to adequately convey what your continued
dedication, devotion and endeavors mean to the health of our wonderful
Fraternity. You have my personal thanks for those efforts and for the many
considerations and kindnesses you’ve shown me.
Thank you again for allowing me to serve as your Grand Lecturer.
Respectfully submitted.
Paul D. Hennig, Grand Lecturer

112

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

APPENDIX G

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

115

GRAND CHAPLAIN’S MESSAGE
by The Very Reverend Maxwell S. Roth
Good morning everyone. Here I am again. The Most Worshipful has
commented that a good speech is a short speech, so with that in mind let’s get
inspired.
I had the wonderful opportunity to travel the state this past year with
the Grand Family. We visited lodges, youth groups and concordant bodies for
cornerstones, anniversary celebrations, receptions in cities large and small, and I
mean really small. This past month we had the pleasure of visiting Knight’s
Ferry, population 300, celebrating Summit Lodge’s 150th anniversary. What I
found inspiring was the love, pride and dedication to Masonry that was evident
everywhere we visited. I would like to publicly acknowledge and thank you for
the wonderful hospitality.
The resurgence of young men petitioning lodges for membership has
been especially gratifying. The enthusiasm that they bring is contagious and
will bring an inspirational thrust to our membership.
It is important that we support our youth groups. I salute the men and
women in our audience this morning who have spent many hours, days, months
and years advising and helping them in their many endeavors. I ask you all to
join in support of our youth groups. Attend some of their meetings and social
activities. You will find it rewarding and they will be grateful for your support.
There are many issues dividing our society today and there is a
tendency for in-your-face confrontations which further polarize us. True
Brethren show respect for the opinions of others and behave with kindness,
patience and respect.
As California has become more diverse in population, so has our
membership. It is wonderful to see members of all races, nationalities and
religions in our Masonic Family. Let us strive to be more sensitive to the
feelings and cultures of all, as it is very easy to unintentionally say or do
something that can be construed differently by others. As Masons, we must
remain true to our values of Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth. We are all on a
common level and are not subjected to the classes and distinction of the outside
world. We know that Freemasonry’s objective is to unite men not to divide
them.
I enjoy a good political joke, if you know a good one email it to me, but
partisan politics should never be in the lodge room or on lodge websites.
Social values are strongly felt; good people can differ in this. These
values are different among religions and even in branches of the same religions.

116

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

It is very important that we remain tolerant of these differences; that is what
makes America and our Masonry strong.
We are the greatest Fraternity in the world so let us build an image in
the minds of the public that Freemasonry is a wonderful institution, a valuable
asset for the community, an influence for public good, dignity, quality and
substance.
The Masonic News writes: “Brethren, be faithful to the promises you
make, the pledges you give, and to the vows you assumed, be faithful to your
family, be faithful to your friends, be faithful to your country, be faithful to
Masonry, thus you will be faithful to yourself and to God.”
Masonry Has Done This
It has led men to aim at a higher and purer life.
It has made men appreciate a higher and nobler manhood.
It has made men to understand better the problems of living.
It has taught men how to die.
It has permeated the whole world with its principles.
It has raised the fallen and rescued the depraved.
It has clothed the naked and fed the hungry.
It has built homes for the sick.
It has educated the ignorant and sheltered the fatherless.
It has helped the church to ameliorate the condition of unhappy humanity.
Its influence has benefited governments in establishing justice and destroying
despotism.
Our Father, source of life, we thank you for the
opportunity and joy that brought us together today in
celebration of our Masonic Family. May this day inspire us to
live our lives as you would wish us to, in love and affection of
our family, friends and flag – according to our tenents of
Freemasonry – Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth. We ask
your blessing on all who are gathered here in our efforts in all
the various spheres of our life. We pray for the healing of our
society, may shrillness and divisiveness give way to tolerance
and goodwill. We ask a special blessing for the men and
women in the service our country walking the distant outposts
of the world and pray for their safe return. With a grateful
heart we acknowledge thy presence in all we do and say today.
May the Lord bless you and keep you. May the Lord
make His face shine upon you, and be gracious unto you.
May the Lord lift up his countenance upon you and give you
peace.”

APPENDIX H

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

119

REPORT OF THE GRAND ORATOR
FREEMASONRY: CHASTE, PURE AND CLEAR
My Brothers all, Good Morning: The first and most important of my
duties as Grand Orator is to thank Most Worshipful Melvyn B. Stein on behalf
of each of the appointed members of the Grand Line, for the trust and
confidence he showed in us to be representatives of our Grand Lodge during the
past 12 months. As we traveled around the state this year, visiting towns big
and small, we had the extraordinary privilege of being with our Grand Master as
he tirelessly, and effectively, delivered his message of Family, Flag and
Freemasonry. With Most Worshipful Mel leading the way, we have seen a
vigorously renewed interest in our Fraternity. It is no accident, or mere
coincidence, that during Most Worshipful Mel’s year as Grand Master, we have
seen such a tremendous increase in the number of young men, whom our Grand
Master refers to as the “millenials,” filing petitions to join our Fraternity. Each
and every member of this Grand Lodge owes a debt of gratitude to our Grand
Master for his outstanding leadership and vision.
On a more personal note, I want to thank Most Worshipful Mel for
giving me a tremendous gift. You see, he gave me much more than a one-year
appointment to the Grand Line. He gave me the gift of Masonic self-realization.
What I realized while traveling around our great state, meeting friends and
brothers from all walks of life, was just how much I love this Fraternity, and
how much Masonry means to me.
There is no assigned topic for any Grand Oration; only the injunction to
address the Grand Lodge on “matters appertaining to the Craft.” I stared with
bewilderment at the vast array of things that could be said, or should be said,
and thought, how could I possibly pick one thing to talk about? So, I did what
many former Grand Orators have done for inspiration—I read all of the grand
orations that have been given at our Annual Communications for the past 150
years.
Some Grand Orators eloquently spoke about the relevance of
Freemasonry to our society; others explained how necessary it is for us to bring
young men into the Fraternity; and still others talked about why we should, or
should not, make changes to our ancient and honorable institution. None of
these orations really helped me choose a topic for this address. Then I thought it
might be a good idea to compare the oration given in 1907 to our way of life 100
years later; so I turned to the oration of Worshipful Jacob Nieto. The records of
the 1907 Annual Communication showed that Worshipful Nieto “delivered a
most thoughtful, scholarly address, which was received with rapt and delighted
attention.” Unfortunately, Brother Nieto delivered his address without notes, and
there is no record of what he actually said. So, there I sat with no inspiration
and no topic, just that absolutely dreadful feeling of standing here and having
nothing to say in front of hundreds of Brothers.

120

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

And then it dawned on me. I was going to be talking to hundreds of
my Brothers—men who had gone through the degrees and worked just as hard
as I had in learning our ritual. So, I thought, what better topic could there be for
an oration than the one thing we all have in common—our love of Freemasonry.
That love is our common bond. It is the reason that we have taken the time to
gather together this weekend.
Freemasonry has given our lives meaning and context. As one
Masonic writer noted, “Freemasonry has existed because it supplies the answer
to many human needs. Man is a social creature and many a non-member is
attracted to Freemasonry by the close friendship that exists between its
members. That elusive tie that binds all masons together actually exists.” I
disagree only slightly with that statement. The tie that binds us together is not
elusive; it is readily apparent. It is our mutual love for our Ancient Craft.
This past July, we had the opportunity to perform a cornerstone
ceremony for the new Reading Masonic Center in Redding, California. It was
during the preparation of my address for that occasion that I had what I now
refer to as my very own “Eureka moment.” Redding, as most of you know, is in
Shasta County, and I was looking for a way to make a tie in between the
location of the new Masonic Center and Freemasonry. In doing my research, I
discovered that among the first pioneers to have seen the as yet unnamed Mount
Shasta was a party of Russian explorers. Upon seeing this pristine mountain, a
mountain that was “clothed with eternal snow,” the Russians called it “Tcheste,”
which means “chaste, pure and clear” and I came to the realization that I love
Freemasonry simply because it is so chaste, so pure and so clear.
With the re-kindled enthusiasm I got from my Eureka moment, I spent
a great deal of time in self-reflection about Freemasonry and my attraction to it.
I became acutely aware, more so than I had ever been before, that Freemasonry
is based entirely on three simple but profound concepts, three universal truths
that form a limitless code of good moral and social behavior. These
fundamental concepts are our belief in, and the practice of, the three principal
tenets of Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth, our belief in and the practice of, the
four cardinal virtues, Temperance, Fortitude, Prudence and Justice, and, the
absolute conviction that all men are created equal. Everything in Masonry stems
from these three concepts. All of the wonderful and intricate ritual we learn, all
of the great lectures we hear, are designed to teach us the meaning of, and how
to practice, this chaste, pure and clear moral code.
As I continued my reflections, I thought a bit of research might be in
order, so I looked up the meanings of “tenet” and “cardinal” to see just how
those words fit into our moral code. A tenet is defined as something that needs
no proof in order to establish its existence--it is known to be true because it is
self-evident that it is true. Well, clearly, the tenets of Brotherly Love, Relief,
and Truth are self-evident universal truths. Next, I found that the word
“cardinal” comes from the Latin “cardo,” which means pivot or hinge. So, it is

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

121

said, the four cardinal virtues of Temperance, Fortitude, Prudence and Justice
form the hinges upon which the door of a moral life swings. When considered
together, our practice of the three principal tenets and the four cardinal virtues
“are what have explained Masonry in the past, what define Masonry today, and
what will distinguish Masonry in the future. They define who we are, what we
stand for and why we are here.” They express, with elegant simplicity, our
chaste, pure and clear reason for existence.
Now, I have long considered myself to be one of the “progressives” in
our Fraternity—always looking for ways to improve our craft and its relevance
to society. I have attended many Masonic installations over the years, and I
used to cringe when I would hear the installing officer admonish the incoming
master that “it is not in the power of any man, or body of men, to make
innovations in the body of Masonry.” But, after my “Eureka moment,” I came
to realize that this admonition is proper because Freemasonry is perfect exactly
the way it is. The three principle tenets and the four cardinal virtues comprise a
philosophy of moral teachings that cannot be improved upon. They are the
body, heart and soul of our Fraternity. They are truths that are self-evident and
in fact cannot be changed--nor should they be changed.
The belief in, and the practice of, the three principal tenets and the four
cardinal virtues, is not something unique to Freemasonry. Philosophers since
the time of Plato have expounded on these themes, and, of course, the ancient
Holy Writings serve as a guide to our understanding and practice of these great
truths. But, as I continued my reflections, I decided that one of the beauties of
Freemasonry is that each one of us finds for himself the context in which he
practices our moral teachings.
In his essay, “The Initiatic Symbolism of Freemasonry,” Wor. Thomas
D. Worrel wrote, “It is not easy to define what Freemasonry is . . . because
Masonry has a deeply individual meaning to each member of the Fraternity. No
doctrine is expounded inside the Lodge; there are no sermons, no interpretations.
Even though teachings are incorporated within [the ritual], the meanings and
interpretations [of the ritual] are for the most part left to the candidate, whose
task it is to integrate them into his own . . . life.”
Of course no one, not even the most ardent admirer of our institution,
can fully and completely put into practice all of our great moral teachings. That
is an impossible task. After all, we are only human beings, with human failings.
But, self-improvement is one of the great goals of our Fraternity and it is in
striving for perfection, striving towards living a perfect Masonic life, that our
goals are achieved. It is the pursuit of becoming better men that actually makes
us better men. And, of course, it is in the Lodge room that we learn how to
become better men. The greatness of Freemasonry lies in the extent we take the
ideals that are taught in the Lodge room, and actually put them into practice in
our daily lives.

122

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

And it was this reflection that brought me back to considering the third
universal truth of Freemasonry, and what is, for me, the most important of all
Masonic teachings--Masonry’s Landmark recognition of the equality of man. I
had often heard the term “Landmarks of Freemasonry,” but I never knew just
exactly what that meant. So as I continued to explore the revelations of my
Eureka moment, I delved into the history of the Landmarks to learn more about
them. According to Albert Mackey, in his famous treatise, Encyclopedia of
Freemasonry, there are 25 Masonic Landmarks. These Landmarks include such
things as the requirement that a man must have a belief in a Supreme Being
before he can be made a Mason; the requirement that the Holy Writings be
present in a tiled Lodge; and the modes of recognition They are called
Landmarks because they are unchanging and unchangeable. But the 25
Landmarks make no mention of the Three Principal Tenets or the Four Cardinal
Virtues. Only one moral Masonic principle is deemed worthy of being called a
Landmark, and that is the principle of “equality.” To paraphrase Mackey, ‘The
doctrine of Masonic equality implies that, as children of one great Father, we
meet in the Lodge upon the level--that on that level we are all traveling to one
predestined goal, that in the Lodge genuine merit shall receive more respect than
boundless wealth, and that virtue and knowledge alone should be the basis of all
Masonic honors.’
As I continued my journey of Masonic self-realization, I came to
understand that my love of Freemasonry stems from the fact that our Fraternity
is, in fact, a great equalizer. We are taught equality from the very first moments
of our initiation. So for me, the Lodge room itself has come to have greater
symbolic meaning than the square and compass, the Holy Writings, or any other
tool or emblem of the Craft, because, more than any other Masonic symbol, the
Lodge room itself represents the ideal of equality.
William Jennings Bryan, perhaps the “grandest” Masonic orator of all,
eloquently explained the emblematic significance of the Lodge room. He said,
“In a Lodge room we do not ask a man who his father was; we simply inquire
what he is. We do not ask what his father has done; we simply ask if he is ready
to do the work that falls to him. We do not ask whether he has received a
diploma from some institution of learning; we simply ask if he has studied the
science of how to live, if he recognizes the ties that bind him to mankind. We
do not ask him how many acres of land he possesses; we ask him whether he is
possessed of the spirit of Brotherhood. The Lodge room helps to draw us
together; it helps to unify the world.”
We all love Freemasonry for different reasons. Some of us revel in the
friendships we form. Others find comfort and serenity in the performance of our
ritual. Still others have found fulfillment in our charitable work. The reasons
we love our Fraternity vary with each one of us. In this, my year of Masonic
self-realization, I have come to understand why I love Freemasonry so much. I

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

123

love it simply because Freemasonry represents the ideal way to live and interact
in our society.
During my 19 years on the bench, I have dealt with some of the worst
behavior that society has to offer. I see so much immoral and unethical conduct
that I sometimes feel myself falling into despair for the future of mankind. It is
then that I turn to the shining beacon of Masonic idealism--that is what keeps me
sane; it is what keeps me grounded; it is what keeps me focused. For me, the
teachings of Freemasonry act as a perfect counter-balance to that roiling
cauldron of misguided souls that I contend with every day. How wonderful it
would be, I think to myself, if everyone attempted to practice Freemasonry to its
fullest extent. The courts would be out of business, and all the judges, and all
the lawyers, would be out of work!
In the end, what I have come to realize most about why I love
Freemasonry, what is most important and fulfilling to me, is the recognition that,
as Freemasons, we are all duty bound to aid, support and protect one another.
We belong to a Fraternity that recognizes the true obligations of brotherhood,
and that is our pure, chaste and clear reason for existence. We fulfill these
obligations by doing for others those things that others need done--And we do
this for the purest of reasons--we do it simply because we are all members of the
same family. We are all children of the one Great Architect of the Universe.
This, my Brothers, is Freemasonry. May it live on through us, and
through our children, for generations to come.
Thank you.
Respectfully submitted,
Ralph B. Dash, Grand Orator

GRAND MASTER’S DECISION AND RECOMMENDATION
Grand Master Stein’s Decision No. 1
Membership in the Snakes
Masonry in California is a brotherhood of men, each of whom has
taken a solemn obligation upon a book of holy writings, to uphold and abide by
all the rules and regulations of our Fraternity. These rules and regulations
include the California Masonic Code.
Section 804.810 of the California Masonic Code provides: “Every
Mason, including a demitted, withdrawn, suspended or expelled Mason, is
subject to all of the duties applicable to him in the California Masonic Code the
General Regulations of Masonry and the Ritual.”
Section 804.825 provides: “It is the duty of every Mason to obey the
Masonic law, as established by the California Masonic Code, the General
Regulations of Masonry and the rights and duties contained in the Ritual.”
Section 805.120(I) provides: “It shall be the duty of the Master to . . .
[c]arefully guard against any infraction, by the members of his Lodge, of its own
Bylaws, the California Masonic Code, the General Regulations of Masonry or
any of the rights or duties set forth in the Ritual . . .”
Section 1200.010 provides: “All members of Masonic Organizations
within this Jurisdiction shall be subject to the same standards of conduct and
propriety as are or may be imposed upon Master Masons in this Jurisdiction.
Neither a Masonic Organization nor any of its members shall, directly or
indirectly, engage in or give any aid or support to any causes, organizations,
business or entertainment schemes or other activities which would reflect
adversely on Masonry or violate this Code.”
Section 1200.015 provides: “A Mason residing in this Jurisdiction shall
not become a member of any Masonic Organization unless:
A.

The Masonic Organization existed in this Jurisdiction on
September 27, 1957; or

B.

The Constitution and Bylaws of the Masonic Organization
have first been submitted to Grand Lodge for approval at a
subsequent Annual Communication.

This section shall not be construed as a recognition of any organization
whether or not in existence on September 27, 1957; nor shall it be construed as
prohibiting clubs organized for the purpose of instituting a new Lodge or
organizations or associations of Past Masters or Masters and Wardens of a
Lodge or Lodges of this Jurisdiction or organizations or associations of Masons

128

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

which do not confer degrees and except for ceremonies of installation, do not
have a ritual other than the Ritual of this Jurisdiction.”
Section 100.060 defines a Masonic Organization as: “Any group,
chapter, order, club, association or organization requiring Masonic affiliation as
a prerequisite to membership, other than a Masonic Lodge.”
Section 1401.010 states: “The following acts constitute unmasonic
conduct sufficient to support the filing of a charge against a Mason:
D.

Any action or failure to act in violation of the requirements of
this Code, the General Regulations of Masonry or the Ritual,
after receipt from the Grand Master of a notice requiring that
Mason to act in accordance with those requirements . . . “

Section 404.130 provides: “The Grand Master shall:
A.
Exercise a general and careful supervision over the Craft, and
see that the requirements of this Code, the General Regulations of
Masonry and the Ritual are strictly maintained, supported and obeyed .
. .”
Pursuant to Section 404.110 and subsequent provisions of the Code, it
is the Grand Master’s responsibility to answer questions as to the meaning of
various provisions in our California Masonic Code by deciding what the law
means and how it applies to the circumstances at hand.
follows:

Based on the foregoing, it is the decision of your Grand Master as
The Grand and Glorious Order of Knights of the Creeping Serpents,
aka “The Snakes” and aka “The Tribu,” is a Masonic Organization as
defined in Sectio 100.060 as it requires Masonic affiliation as a
prerequisite to its membership and does not constitute a Masonic
Lodge.
The aforesaid Masonic Organization did not exist in this jurisdiction on
September 27, 1957 nor have the Constitution and Bylaws of said
Masonic Organization been submitted to the Grand Lodge for approval
as required by Section 1200.015.
The aforesaid Masonic Organization does have a ritual other than the
Ritual of this Jurisdiction.
Pursuant to Section 1200.010, members of the aforesaid Organization
within this Jurisdiction are subject to the same standards of conduct and
propriety as are or may be imposed upon Master Masons in this
Jurisdiction. Because the existence of the aforesaid Organization
violates this Code, members of the aforesaid Masonic Organization and
the Masonic Organization itself are in violation of Section 1200.015.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

129

The Grand and Glorious Order of Knights of the Creeping Serpents,
aka “The Snakes” aka “The Tribu,” is an organization in violation of
the California Masonic Code and all the members of said Masonic
Organization within California and all other Masons residing in this
Jurisdiction are directed not to become a member of this Organization
and may not remain a member of said Organization until said
Organization shall have complied with the requirements of Section
1200.015 of the California Masonic Code.
All members of the aforesaid Masonic Organization within this
Jurisdiciton (pursuant to Section 1200.101) and all Master Masons of

provisions of the California Masonic Code by being members of the

Mason pursuant to Section 1401.010.D after receipt of proper notice.

Section 404.130 of the California Masonic Code provides, "The Grand
Master shall: "A. Exercise a general and careful supervision over the Craft and
see that the requirements of this Code, the General Regulations of Masonry and
the Ritual are strictly maintained, supported and obeyed..." Section 804.825
provides "It is the duty of every Mason to obey the Masonic law, as established
by the California Masonic Code, the General Regulations of Masonry and the

become a member of any Masonic organizations unless:

"A. The Masonic Organization existed in the Jurisdiction on September
27, 1957; or
B. The Constitution and Bylaws of the Masonic Organization have first

The Grand Master, in his letter accompanying his Decision gives
background, as to what has become an insubordinate situation within our Grand
Lodge with respect to an organization called "The Grand and Glorious Order
Knights of the Creeping Serpents" (also known as "The Snakes" or "The Tribu")
. The Grand Master has determined that the members of this organization have
maintained their membership and activity despite the fact that they were
committing violations of the California Masonic Code. The organization did not
exist in California before September 27, 1957 and its Constitution and Bylaws
of the Masonic Organization have not been submitted to the Grand Lodge for
approval at any subsequent Annual Communication. The Grand Master has
indicated that the organization is actually in many respects working contrary to
the Grand Lodge of California and its subordinate organizations. The Grand
Master's Decision is based on the information supplied to him and he in turn has
made a proper decision under our Masonic Code.
This Decision is consistent with the policy and general purposes of this
Grand Lodge.
Decision AFFIRMED.

Grand Master Stein’s Recommendation No. 1
Extending Deadlines Which Fall on Weekends and Holidays
Section 403.040 of the California Masonic Code requires all
resolutions to be presented to the Grand Secretary at least 180 days prior to the
commencement date of the next Annual Communication. Because the 2007
Annual Communication commenced on Friday, September 28th, the deadline fell
this year on Sunday, April 1st. Some resolution proponents assumed incorrectly
that the deadline would move to the next business day, and they submitted their
legislation on Monday, April 2nd which was after the deadline.
Our law contains no provision which allows a deadline otherwise
falling on a weekend or a holiday to be moved to the next business day.
California state law makes such an allowance, and I think that our California
Masonic Code should include a similar provision.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

131

If you adopt this recommendation, a new Section 300.050 will be added
to the Code to read as follows:

§300.050. DEADLINES ON WEEKENDS OR HOLIDAYS.
Whenever any act is appointed by this Code to be performed upon a particular
day, which day falls upon a Saturday, Sunday or day recognized by the federal
government as a holiday, it may be performed upon the next business day, with
the same effect as if it had been performed upon the day appointed.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
In Recommendation 2007-01, the Grand Master proposes to avoid
future ambiguity with respect to any deadline prescribed in our Masonic Code
which would be occasioned by such deadline falling on a Holiday or Weekend.
Notwithstanding the hazard one faces by waiting until the 11th hour to conform
to legislated deadlines, the Grand Master shows reason and compassion by
recommending that we model our Code to parallel California State Law when
defining our deadlines, thus removing any hint of uncertainty for those
interpreting our meaning of the word “deadline.”
We have a long history of revising our Masonic Code to “simplify and
clarify” our laws for the good of the order.
This recommendation is consistent with the policies and general
purposes of this Grand Lodge.
Recommendation APPROVED.

132

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

CARRY-OVER FROM 2006 ANNUAL COMMUNICATION
Grand Master Sorsabal’s Recommendation No. 1
Reducing 5/6 to 3/4 for Adoption of Legislation
The California Masonic Code now requires that legislation which
changes the code receive a 5/6 vote in order to be adopted. If it receives at least
a majority, but less than the 5/6, the legislation is carried over to the next year,
when it can be adopted by a 2/3 vote. The purpose of this rule is to ensure that
our law is not easily changed, or at least not as easily changed the first year that
it is considered. While this rule has served us well over the years, it seems to
me as Grand Master that the 5/6 rule is excessive. To require an affirmative
vote of 83-1/3% of those voting at the Annual Communication to adopt changes
in our basic law is excessively high. I am therefore proposing that we change
the 5/6 rule to a 3/4 rule. Seventy-five percent of those voting is still a very high
number, and one that will still ensure that a strong consensus exists before new
legislation is adopted. It takes 3/4 of the states to amend the United States
Constitution; it makes sense that it should take a 3/4 vote of our Grand Lodge to
amend ours.
If you adopt this recommendation, the following changes will be made
to the Code. Section 820 of the Code would be amended to read as marked:

§820. REQUIRED VOTE.
Following the report of the Committee on Jurisprudence and all committees to
which any proposed amendment has been referred, the vote shall be taken. If
five-sixths three-quarters of the vote is in favor of the proposed amendment, it
shall be adopted and at the close of that Communication, it shall become a part
of this Constitution.
If the vote in favor of a proposed amendment is less than five-sixths threequarters but there is a majority, it shall lie over for one year, and shall be
published with the Proceedings, under the caption of “Proposed Amendment to
the Constitution.” If, at the next succeeding Annual Communication, it receives
two-thirds of the votes it shall be adopted. At the close of that Communication
it shall become a part of this Constitution.
Section 34020 of the Code would be amended to read as marked:

§34020. REQUIRED VOTE.
Following the report of the Committee on Jurisprudence and all committees to

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

133

which a proposed amendment has been referred, the vote shall be taken. If
five-sixths three-quarters of the votes are in favor of the proposed amendment, it
shall be adopted and, from and at the close of that Communication, shall become
a part of these Ordinances.
If the vote in favor of a proposed amendment be less than five-sixths threequarters but there is a majority, it shall lie over for one year, and shall be
published with the Proceedings under the caption of “Proposed Amendment to
the Ordinances.” If, at the next succeeding Annual Communication, it receives
two-thirds of the votes, it shall be declared adopted. At the close of that
Communication, it shall become a part of these Ordinances.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Last year, Grand Master Sorsabal recommended a change to Section
820, now section 50.000, of the California Masonic Code, dealing with the vote
required before a change in the Code may be made. Currently it takes a 5/6,
approximately 83%, vote of the membership to pass any proposed legislation.
Grand Master Sorsabal believes that this percentage is excessive and is
recommending that it be reduced to 3/4, or 75% of the membership, in order to
pass new legislation, so that our Code may be more easily amended.
This recommendation does not change our current procedure with
respect to carryovers; we would still carry-over legislation that receives a simple
majority vote, regardless of whether the required vote for outright passage is 5/6
or 3/4. All carry-overs would still require a 2/3 vote of the membership for
passage at the following Annual Communication.
The policy of Grand Lodge has always been never to amend our Code
without the assurance that the membership strongly supports the change. Grand
Master Sorsabal states that even at a 3/4 - 75% vote requirement, passage of new
legislation would still show that a strong consensus of the membership exists on
any new legislation adopted.
It should be noted that at least eight times in prior years, going back to
1965, it has been proposed that the California Masonic Code be amended to
reduce to 2/3 – 67%, the amount needed to pass legislation the first time it is
presented. In every case, this proposal has failed. However, if 75% of the
membership is in favor of the proposed legislation, there is a stronger consensus
that the proposed legislation should become part of our law.
This recommendation is consistent with the policy and general
purposes of this Grand Lodge.
Recommendation NOT ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 06-03
Lodge Formation
The proponents of this Resolution seek to reduce the minimum required
members for a new lodge form 25 to 15.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Grand Lodge historically chartered four or five lodges
annually and it has been more than a quarter century since our jurisdiction has
performed at this level; and
WHEREAS, the development of new lodges is parallel to the
prosperity and continuance of California Freemasonry. Cities absent of Masonic
lodges cannot raise members or Masonicly benefit the local population in
general; and
WHEREAS, of the 515 cities in California, 230 currently have no
Masonic presence; and
WHEREAS, there are 152 California cities with populations greater
than 20,000 and 36 California cities that have populations greater than 50,000
that are currently lacking a Masonic lodge; and
WHEREAS, in an era of declining membership it is necessary to take
steps to facilitate the chartering of new lodges by reducing the number of Master
Masons required to form a new lodge; and
WHEREAS, California Freemasonry will benefit from encouraging
special interest or “affinity” lodges that my have small memberships, composed
of Brethren from various backgrounds and interests such as: firefighters,
educators, servicemen, or other specific ethnic and/or cultural interests.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 14010 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§14010. PETITION FOR DISPENSATION.
Upon the petition of at least 25 15 and not more than 50 Master Masons in good
standing, the Grand Master may grant them a dispensation to open and hold a
Lodge at the place named therein, under such name as shall be approved by him,
with power to make Masons and receive members by affiliation. He shall
appoint the Master and Wardens of the new Lodge, provided that the Grand
Secretary has certified to the Grand Master that each signer on the petition has
presented either a:
A.

Dimit;

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

135

B.

Dues receipt accompanied by a certified statement from the Secretary
of his Lodge that a Dimit will be issued on notification that the
requested dispensation has been issued;

C.

Dues receipt from members of Lodges in this Jurisdiction accompanied
by either:

D.

1.

A demand for transfer of membership, or

2.

A statement of the petitioner’s desire to affiliate as a plural
member; or

Certificate of Good Standing and a statement of the petitioner’s desire
to affiliate as a dual member in the case of a member belonging in a
Jurisdiction which recognizes dual membership.

Whenever this Section requires a dues receipt be presented, a photocopy thereof,
duly certified by the Secretary of any Lodge in this Jurisdiction, may be
substituted.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to change Section 14010, now
section 801.015, of the California Masonic Code to require 15, instead of 25,
Master Masons as the minimum number necessary to petition the Grand Master
for a dispensation to open and hold a new Lodge.
The proponents state that very few Lodges have been constituted in the
past 25 years. This is true. In fact, there have been many consolidations, even
charters surrendered, during this period. In the areas of the state where this has
occurred, Brethren either have become active in the newly consolidated Lodge,
or became dormant members because of the distances they had to travel to
attend meetings. Many of these former Lodges were in the 230 California cities
that now have no Masonic presence.
While California currently requires no less than 25 Master Masons to
petition for a dispensation to form a Lodge, many other Jurisdictions require
less. For instance, Alaska requires only 15, North Carolina 20, Ohio 12, Maine 7
and our Mother Grand Lodge, the United Grand Lodge of England, requires
only 7. Under this Resolution, the Grand Master would still have the right and
duty to ensure that any new Lodge would be financially viable.
Masonry is on the rise in California. While overall membership has
declined, the number of new petitions received has been increasing annually. It
is the policy of this Grand Lodge to facilitate membership, and this Resolution
would help accomplish that goal.

136

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

This Resolution is consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Finance:
The cost for the creation of a new lodge includes the instituting of the
new lodge by the Grand Master, and the constituting of the new lodge by the
Grand Master after a charter has been approved. If this legislation makes it
easier to form a new lodge, it might be assumed that more new lodges would be
created, and thus there would be a need for more such ceremonies. We estimate
that each ceremony costs Grand Lodge approximately $2,000. Since two
ceremonies per lodge are required, each new lodge would cost Grand Lodge
$4,000. Thus a new lodge of 15 members would pay $412.50 at the current per
capita rate of $27.50. The $4,000 is a “one time” expense.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 06-05
Forfeiture of Fees
The proponents of this Resolution seek to reinstate the rule for forfeiture of fees
and contributions for failure to advance.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
BE IT RESOLVED that Section 26330 of the California Masonic
Code be amended as marked:

§26330.
RESTORATION OF PREVIOUSLY FORFEITED FEES
FORFEITURE OF FEES AND CONTRIBUTIONS.
Any Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft who failed to present himself for
advancement to the next higher degree, and whose fees and contributions shall have
been forfeited for failure to advance in accordance with the laws of this Jurisdiction
previously in effect, shall have all such fees and contributions restored as if no
action to forfeit these fees had ever taken place. He shall be restored automatically
as an Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft, as the case may be, as if the forfeiture
had never taken place, and shall be entitled to advancement by presenting himself
to the Lodge and complying with the provisions of Section 26340 of this Code.
When a candidate does not present himself qualified for the Fellow Craft and
Master Mason Degrees within a period of three years from the date of his

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

137

initiation as an Entered Apprentice, the Master shall appoint a committee of two
or more members of the Lodge to investigate and report back to the Master the
reasons why the candidate has not presented himself for advancement. If the
candidate does not supply any explanation satisfactory to the Lodge, the Master
shall direct the Secretary to forward to such candidate either by registered letter
or certified mail, with return receipt requested, deposited in the U.S. Post Office,
addressed to him at his last known address, a notice advising him that unless he
shall present himself qualified for advancement, on or before a specified date (at
least 60 days in advance), his fees for the unconferred degrees and all of his
previous contributions shall be declared forfeited.
On the date specified, the Master shall declare the fees and contributions
forfeited unless further time shall have been granted by a vote of the Lodge.
A candidate thus denied advancement shall be considered only as an Entered
Apprentice or Fellow Craft of the Lodge with the right to apply for the
remaining degree or degrees, and again paying the fees and contributions
therefor, subject to investigation and election, the same as any other candidate.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Under current provisions of the California Masonic Code, no time limit
is set for an Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft to present himself for
advancement to a higher degree. The Lodge in which this non-advancing
candidate originally sought his degrees is required to account for and maintain,
for an indefinite period, all fees such candidate has paid. In years past, the
California Masonic Code had provided for the forfeiture of fees for a candidate’s
failure to advance. However, at the Annual Communication in 1997, Section
26330, now section 804.690, was amended, restoring all such fees as if no
forfeiture had occurred. Accordingly, Lodges have been required to carry these
non-advancing candidates on the Lodge books for many years. This Resolution
would amend California Masonic Code section 804.690 so that if the Entered
Apprentice or Fellow Craft failed to present himself for advancement for a
period of three years, his fees would be forfeited. If the Entered Apprentice or
Fellow Craft seeks to advance after his fees have been forfeited, he would have
to pay them again.
While it has long been the policy of this Grand Lodge to encourage the
orderly advancement of candidates, it is also its policy to simplify the
administrative work of the constituent Lodges. If a candidate fails to present
himself for advancement after three years, allowing the forfeiture of his fees
after this period of time would ease the administrative work of the Lodge by
permitting the candidate to be dropped from the Lodge rolls. Nothing, other
than the repayment of his degree fees, would prohibit a candidate from seeking
advancement after his fees have been forfeited, so long as he complies with the
provisions of section 804.620.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

This Resolution is consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 06-09
Formulation of Annual Dues of Constituent Lodges
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow for rounding up of Lodge dues
based in part on the annual Grand Lodge per capita.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the current California Masonic Code allows for the
constituent Lodges to specify in their By-Laws that their annual dues be either a
fixed amount or a fixed amount plus the Per Capita sum approved by Grand
Lodge at the immediately preceding Annual Communication; and
WHEREAS, using the latter form is attractive because it conveniently
relieves each Lodge from the need to increase its dues whenever the Per Capita
sum is increased by Grand Lodge; and
WHEREAS, using the latter form would still require each Lodge to
annually adjust the dues of each member and could potentially result in such a
strange and unfriendly total value as $63.57; and
WHEREAS, being able to round the total to some more reasonable
value and have it remain at that value for a reasonable period, would appear
more prudent to the brethren and would be advantageous for the Secretaries.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 1 of Article
IX of the Uniform Lodge Bylaws set forth in Section 19040 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:
Section 1. The dues of each member of this Lodge shall be $_____ per annum,
payable in advance on the first day of January.
Or in the alternative
The dues of each member of this Lodge shall be the sum of $______ plus the
Per Capita sum approved by Grand Lodge at the immediately preceding Annual
Communication, per annum, payable in advance on the first day of January.
Or in the alternative

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

139

The dues of each member of this Lodge shall be the sum of $ ______ plus the
Per Capita sum approved by Grand Lodge at the immediately preceding Annual
Communication, with the total thus determined rounded up to the next higher
whole multiple of $ ______, per annum, payable in advance on the first day of
January.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow for the “rounding up”
of Lodge dues when those dues result in a figure that would be less than a whole
dollar amount, as distinguished from what the proponents call a “strange and
unfriendly total value [such] as $63.57.” Presently, a Lodge is permitted to
charge a fixed, whole dollar sum, for dues, a sum which includes the Grand
Lodge per capita charge. In the alternative, a Lodge may require its members to
pay a base amount for dues, then add to it the Grand Lodge per capita. It is this
latter formula which may result in a “strange and unfriendly total.”
The rounding up of dues, by a matter of cents, would make Lodge
accounting easier, without creating any serious financial hardship to Lodge
members who pay their dues on a yearly basis. Thus, so long as the Lodge has
provided for the payment of its full per capita assessment, this Resolution makes
sense, spelled “s-e-n-s-e.”
This Resolution is consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.
Resolution ADOPTED.

140

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

2007 RESOLUTIONS
Resolution No. 07-01
Allow Inspectors To Vote At Annual Communication
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow each Inspector a separate vote
in Grand Lodge.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Inspectors, acting as they do, as the connection between
constituent lodges and Grand Lodge, are uniquely situated to observe trends
developing within their districts; and
WHEREAS, as a consequence, their close up observation bring a view
that is currently not represented in Grand Lodge. Grand Lodge officers
represent views that are state-wide in perspective, whereas the constituent
lodges represent views that are entirely local in nature. By adding Inspectors to
the voting “mix,” the electorate will then include area representatives that could
serve to better balance legislative actions taken at Grand Lodge; and
WHEREAS, since there are currently 102 Inspectors, and the total
number of voting members is already 1,431, the addition of Inspectors would
not result in a massive change to the electorate, but would, rather, simply serve
to make it better balanced.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 403.075 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§403.075. VOTES.
Except as provided in Sections 403.080 and 403.085 of this Code, the following
shall each be entitled to one vote only:
A.

Elective and appointive Grand Lodge Officers except the Grand Tiler
and the Assistant Grand Tiler;

B.

Past Grand Officers;

C.

Inspectors;

D.

Masters, Senior Wardens and Junior Wardens of chartered Lodges; and

D.E.

Past Masters collectively of each chartered Lodge.

If neither the Master nor either of the Wardens of a Lodge has registered, the
elected representative of the Lodge may cast the votes of Master and Wardens of
the Lodge.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

141

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that Section 403.080 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§403.080. CUMULATIVE VOTING.
If one or more of the Master and Wardens of a Lodge has not registered, or has
registered and is not present, the senior such officer registered, in addition to his
own vote, may also cast the vote of the absentee or absentees. If an incumbent
officer of Grand Lodge, or a Past Grand Officer or an Inspector is also a Master,
Warden or elected representative of a Lodge, he may cast all votes to which
such positions entitle him, but he shall not be entitled to cast the vote afforded
the Past Masters of a Lodge. If the elected representative is a Past Master but
not an incumbent officer of Grand Lodge, or a Past Grand Officer or an
Inspector, he may also cast the vote afforded the Past Masters of a Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of Resolution 2007-01 believe that the Inspectors
represent a more general perspective of California Masonry than do the
individual Lodges. Since their role is to act as a liaison between the Lodges and
Grand Lodge, Inspectors do have an interest in the legislation that is voted on at
the Annual Communication. As such, the proponents believe that Inspectors
should have voting privileges. Furthermore, these proponents believe that, since
the number of Inspectors is only a small percentage of the total number of
potential votes that would be cast, they would not be able, by themselves, to
influence the final outcome in any significant way.
Currently ballots are distributed to all elected Masters, Senior Wardens
and Junior Wardens who attend Grand Lodge. In addition, the Past Masters
from each Lodge are provided one collective ballot. All current Grand Lodge
elected and appointed officers, with the exception of the Grand Tiler and
Assistant Grand Tiler, have one vote each. Finally, each past elected grand
officer in attendance is given a ballot. Thus, there is precedent in our code for
men who were either appointed to an office, or even not appointed or elected to
any office in the current Masonic year, to cast a ballot.
An Inspector is the official representative of the Grand Master in his
particular Masonic District. In this role, he does become, at least in part, the
functional equivalent of a Grand Lodge officer.
As of June 27, 2007, there were 99 Inspectorial Districts listed in the
Grand Lodge roster. As an extreme example of the influence that they might
exert, had all 99 Inspectors been able to vote, and did so in a block for the
minority side, 6 of the 19 pieces of legislation that were considered at the 2006

142

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

annual communication would have had a different fate. It must be emphasized
that, while this would not be an insignificant effect, this is a worst-case scenario,
as all 99 Inspectors would be unlikely to be in attendance and to vote the same
way on every issue.
The Inspectors do bring a different perspective to the voting. Their
primary interest is in a broader area than the individual Lodges, but not as broad
an area as Grand Lodge as a whole. This is a unique position that, at this time,
is not represented by any other individual who is accorded a vote in Grand
Lodge deliberations. In the past, the policy of Grand Lodge has been to obtain
as many perspectives as possible. The addition of Inspectors to the voting pool
would allow their unique perspective to be included in any vote of the Grand
Lodge.
This Resolution is consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.
Resolution CARRIED OVER.

Resolution No. 07-02
Past Master’s Title When Serve Consecutive Terms
The proponents of this Resolution seek to confer the title of Past Master to a
brother who succeeds himself in office as Master.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the title of Past Master is earned by many years of
devotion to one’s Lodge; and
WHEREAS, an extra measure of devotion is required for a brother to
serve a second consecutive year as Master; and
WHEREAS, Section 805.020 C is ambiguous in its definition in
respect to the term “successor.”
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 805.020 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§805.020. PAST MASTERS.
The title of Past Master applies only to a member who:
A.

Has been regularly elected or named in a Charter as Master of a
chartered Lodge in this Jurisdiction (other than a Research Lodge);

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

143

B.

Has been installed as Master of a chartered Lodge in this Jurisdiction
(other than a Research Lodge);

C.

Has served until the installation of his successor, except in the case of
death or consolidation, as provided below, or has served until his own
installation as Master of such Lodge for the subsequent year; and

D.

Is at the time in good standing in a Constituent Lodge in this
Jurisdiction.

If a Master resigns before the installation of his successor, he forfeits all right to
the title of Past Master and to membership in Grand Lodge, unless otherwise
qualified.
If a member has been regularly elected or named in a Charter and installed as
Master of a chartered Lodge in this Jurisdiction and dies while serving as
Master, he may by resolution of his Lodge be designated as a Past Master.
A member in good standing in a Constituent Lodge in this Jurisdiction who has
been regularly elected or named in a Charter and installed as Master of a
chartered Lodge in this Jurisdiction shall have the title of Past Master, if the
Lodge consolidated while he was serving as Master and he did not become
Master of the consolidated Lodge.
A member in good standing in a Constituent Lodge in this Jurisdiction who has
been regularly elected or named in a Charter, installed as Master and served
until the installation of his successor in a chartered Lodge in another Jurisdiction
may take the honorary title of “Past Master,” but he shall not thereby become a
member of this Grand Lodge.
If Grand Lodge charters a Lodge formed by a Grand Body recognized by this
Grand Lodge, it may by resolution provide that the title of Past Master shall
apply to the Past Masters of such Lodge and entitle them to the rights and
privileges of Past Masters of this Jurisdiction.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of Resolution No. 2007-02 seek to clarify the
application of the title of Past Master, when the sitting Master succeeds himself
as the incoming Master. Once a Master has served his full term of one year, he is
then considered a Past Master. If he is re-elected to serve a second consecutive
term, he is a Past Master having served one year as Master, but is still called
Master of his Lodge because he is the sitting ranking Officer.
This proposed resolution clarifies the current language used in the
California Masonic Code so there is no confusion regarding when the title of
“Past Master” may be used.

This Resolution is consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 07-03
Grand Treasurer’s Report At Annual Communication
The proponents of this Resolution seek to require from the Grand Treasurer an
interim financial report at Grand Lodge as of July 31st of the current Grand
Lodge fiscal year.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Section 404.230 F of the California Masonic Code
requires the Grand Treasurer to deliver at each Annual Communication “a
detailed report of the financial condition of Grand Lodge, prepared in
accordance with generally accepted accounting principles on the accrual method
of accounting, using a fiscal year ending October 31st;” and
WHEREAS, the Code further requires that this report “shall have been
examined by the independent public accountant of Grand Lodge as provided in
Section 407.045 of this Code, submitted to the Committee on Finance, and shall
be published in the Proceedings of Grand Lodge;” and
WHEREAS, it is desirable that the Grand Treasurer present a report on
the current state of Grand Lodge finances at the Annual Communication, even
though the official report for the current fiscal year will not be issued until after
the close of the current fiscal year, and after the completion of the audit.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 404.230 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§404.230. GRAND TREASURER.
The Grand Treasurer shall be the chief financial officer of the Grand Lodge. It
shall be the duty of the Grand Treasurer:
A.

To receive all monies belonging to Grand Lodge from the Grand
Secretary, giving his receipt therefor, and, immediately upon the receipt
of said monies, to deposit the same to the credit of Grand Lodge;

B.

Take charge of all other property of Grand Lodge, except property
specifically charged to the Grand Secretary;

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

145

C.

Disburse funds for all appropriations by Grand Lodge as provided in
Section 407.035 of this Code;

D.

Keep an accurate, complete and uniform set of financial records,
accounting for all receipts, disbursements, assets and liabilities of
Grand Lodge. Such records shall be kept on the accrual basis of
accounting and maintained in accordance with generally accepted
accounting principles;

E.

Attend Grand Lodge at all its Communications, except Ceremonial
Communications, and to make available to the Grand Master, when
required, the books and all necessary records appertaining to his office;
and also, if required by Grand Lodge or the Grand Master, to provide
such books and records to any committee which may be appointed to
act in relation to the fiscal affairs of Grand Lodge;

F.

Prepare annually and deliver, at each Annual Communication, a
detailed report of the financial condition of Grand Lodge, prepared in
accordance with generally accepted accounting principles on the
accrual method of accounting, using a fiscal year ending October 31st.
Such report shall have been examined by the independent public
accountant of Grand Lodge as provided in Section 407.045 of this
Code, submitted to the Committee on Finance, and shall be published
in the Proceedings of Grand Lodge. In addition, the Grand Treasurer
shall prepare and deliver, at each Annual Communication, an interim,
unaudited report of the financial condition of Grand Lodge as of July
31st of the current Grand Lodge fiscal year;

G.

Be responsible for the management of the personnel of the Accounting
Section of the Grand Lodge office and to create and adopt job
descriptions for and define the duties of said personnel, however, they
are subject to the general administration of the Grand Secretary;

H.

Pay over and transfer to his successor at the end of his term all funds
and property of Grand Lodge then in his possession; and

I.

Perform such other duties as are set forth elsewhere in this Code or as
may be requested by the Grand Master or Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Resolution 2007-03 is basically one of procedure to enable the Grand Treasurer
to prepare a "detailed report of the financial condition at the end of the current Grand
Lodge fiscal year."
This Resolution requires the Grand Treasurer to give an interim report, not a
final report, to the Grand Lodge at its Annual Communication. Since the date of the
Annual Communication may be changed from time to time, this Resolution permits

146

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

the Grand Treasurer to present an interim report to the Grand Lodge, and then at the end of
the fiscal year prepare that detailed report.
The necessity of this Resolution is the uncertainty of the timing of the Annual
Communication each year and the inability of the Grand Treasurer therefore to give a
full Grand Lodge fiscal year accounting at the Annual Communication. This Resolution
enables him to give an interim report to the Grand Lodge and then complete his
final report at the appropriate time.
This legislation will assist in the proper administration of the activities of the
Grand Lodge of California. It will permit the Grand Treasurer to report the information
available to the membership of the Grand Lodge duly assembled and then complete his
task as required as of the end of the fiscal year.
This Resolution is consistent with the policy and general purposes of this
Grand Lodge.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 07-04
Qualification Procedures For Past Masters
The proponents of this Resolution seek to change and clarify the qualifications
required of a Past Master should he be elected to serve as Master, Senior
Warden or Junior Warden of his Lodge.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the number of Masons in California has decreased by
more than 120,000 in the past 25 years, representing a decrease of over 60%;
and
WHEREAS, many Lodges have been forced to consolidate or
surrender their charter due to lack of qualified officers to run the Lodge; and
WHEREAS, our shrinking numbers have forced many Past Masters to
step back into line to keep the line of officers intact; and
WHEREAS, a Past Master returning to the line must re-qualify, not
only for the work of the station he is assuming but also for all stations requiring
qualification below the state he is assuming (for example, a Past Master
returning to the station of Senior Warden must re-qualify for all the work of the
Senior Deacon, Junior Warden and the work of the Senior Warden); and
WHEREAS, this qualification process places an extreme burden on a
member who has previously qualified for all these stations; and

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

147

WHEREAS, many Past Masters will not consider returning to the line
because of this onerous re-qualification process; and
WHEREAS, those who have served as Master of their lodge in the
recent past are still very familiar with the work; and
WHEREAS, in many Masonic districts throughout California the
provisions of Code Sections 805.520, 805.530 and 805.540 are not being
uniformly enforced.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 805.520 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§805.520.
ELECT.

CONDITIONS TO INSTALLATION OF THE MASTER-

Before a Master-elect can be installed, there shall be presented to the Installing
Officer a Certificate of Qualification of the Grand Lecturer, the Assistant Grand
Lecturer of the division in which his Lodge is situated, or the Inspector of the
district in which his Lodge is situated, certifying that:
A.

He has personally examined the Master-elect and that he is qualified to
give the work and lectures in the Entered Apprentice, Fellow Craft and
Master Mason Degrees. If the Master-elect has qualified and served as
Master of a Constituent Lodge in this Jurisdiction at any time during
the six year period preceding his election, he shall not be required to
qualify to give the work and lectures in the Entered Apprentice or
Fellow Craft Degrees;

B.

He has received a Certificate of Proficiency in the Candidate’s Lecture
of the Master Mason Degree in a Constituent Lodge of this Jurisdiction
at any time since he was raised; and

C.

He is proficient in those portions of this Code that relate to the
government of a Lodge.

Immediately after the installation, the Installing Officer shall endorse the
Master’s Certificate of Qualification and forward it to the Grand Secretary.
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that Section 805.530 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§805.530.
CONDITIONS TO INSTALLATION OF THE SENIOR
WARDEN-ELECT.

Installing Officer a Certificate of Qualification of the Grand Lecturer, the

He has personally examined the Senior Warden-elect and that he is
