§805.540.
CONDITIONS TO INSTALLATION OF THE JUNIOR
WARDEN-ELECT.
Before a Junior Warden-elect can be installed, there shall be presented to the
Installing Officer a Certificate of Qualification of the Grand Lecturer, the
Assistant Grand Lecturer of the division in which his Lodge is situated, or the
Inspector of the district in which his Lodge is situated, certifying that:
A.

He has personally examined the Junior Warden-elect and that he is
qualified to give the work and lecture of the Entered Apprentice
Degree;

B.

He has received a Certificate of Proficiency in the Candidate’s Lecture
of the Master Mason Degree in a Constituent Lodge of this Jurisdiction
at any time since he was raised; and

C.

He is proficient in those portions of this Code that relate to the
government of a Lodge.

Immediately after the installation, the Installing Officer shall endorse the Junior
Warden’s Certificate of Qualification and forward it to the Grand Secretary.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

149

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of Resolution No. 2007-04 seek to change the
qualification procedures for a Master-elect, Senior Warden-elect, or Junior
Warden-elect who has served as Master within the six years prior to his election.
This Resolution would eliminate the requirement that these officers-elect must
demonstrate their proficiency to handle the ritual of their respective offices. Our
rules for qualification are straightforward and are in place to ensure that senior
officers are proficient in the ritual. Just because a Past Master has qualified
once before does not mean that he remains proficient in the ritual. Past Masters
have a wealth of knowledge and should not be discouraged from returning to the
officer corps if needed, but they too should demonstrate current proficiency in
the ritual, as is now required by the California Masonic Code.
Four times over the past 10 years, Resolutions similar to this one have
been proposed and have failed.
This Resolution is not consistent with the policy and general purposes
of this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Ritual:
Brethren of the Grand Lodge, Resolution No. 2007-04 entitled
“Qualification Procedures for Past Masters” seeks to accomplish two things: (1)
to modify the current manner of qualifying Lodge Officers by allowing Brethren
who have served as Master within the previous six (6) years to be able to qualify
again as pedestal officers by demonstrating ONLY their current level of
proficiency in the work and lectures of the position which they are attempting to
fill; and (2) to modify the CMC to require those seeking to become pedestal
officers to have a California Master Mason’s proficiency certificate.
The Ritual Committee does not object to the second part of the
resolution as it believes pedestal officers need to be proficient in the California
Candidate’s Lecture of the Master Mason Degree as opposed to the Candidate’s
Lecture from other jurisdictions.
However, the first portion of the resolution presents a most
controversial view and deserves detailed discussion.
The proposal of providing Past Masters with special qualification
privileges has been a topic of discussion during prior sessions of the Grand
Lodge.
In 1989 the Policy and General Purposes Committee noted that “The
senior officers of the Lodge, the Master in particular, are charged with the
responsibility of overseeing all of the activities in the lodge, including the ritual.
As such, they themselves must be proficient in the ritual. If someone wants to

150

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

sit in the east and judge the proficiency level of another, his own knowledge
must be complete and thorough.”
In 1997 in response to a resolution intended to exempt officers from requalifying for a TWO YEAR period, the Ritual Committee observed that
holding a certificate attesting that a man was proficient at one time does not
mean that he is forever proficient.
In 2000 the Grand Lodge discussed theissue of whether or not to allow
Past Masters to “automatically qualify” in the lectures of the three degrees when
holding offices of Master, Senior Warden or Junior Warden. Additionally, both
the Ritual and the Policy and General Purposes Committees provided opinions
in support of maintaining the current practice of requiring all Brothers,
INCLUDING PAST MASTERS, who aspire to be Master of their Lodge to be
treated equally by demonstrating to the Inspector their respective current level of
proficiency in the office they are about to assume. In each of the prior attempts
the members of Grand Lodge voted overwhelmingly against special
qualification privileges for Past Masters.
This resolution also establishes a two-tier system for men who desire to
become Masters of their ldoges, an act that is contrary to the equalitarian nature
of Masonry.
Currently, Past Masters who wish to return to service as Master, Senior
or Junior Warden, or Senior Deacon must “re-qualify” in the position to which
they are returning in order to demonstrate to the District Inspector BOTH their
administrative and ritual abilities to perform the duties of the office they are
about to “reassume.” The purpose of the “re-qualification” is to ensure the
concerned Brother has retained sufficient administrative and ritual information
so that he will be able to PROPERLY perform his duties. In the case of the
Master and Senior Warden both positions require the officer to be proficient in
the duties of the lesser position.
Once a Master becomes a Past Master he is expected to receive a welldeserved rest, or that is the way it is supposed to work. However we all know
that too often recently made Past Masters and all too often the long-time Past
Masters are pressed into service to fill vacant positions such as Senior Deacon,
Junior Warden, Senior Warden and even Master.
Fortunately, some Past Masters have not been immediately pressed into
such service and have enjoyed a time where they were able to observe and not
practice their administrative and/or ritual skills. As such, over time, both the
administrative and ritual skills of these Past Masters naturally weaken, and in
some cases, weaken more than others.
There are three types of Past Masters, those that continue to attend
Lodge regularly and retain administrative and ritual proficiency by practicing
their work, those that attend Lodge regularly and do not practice their work, and

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

151

those that do attend Lodge regularly. In each case administrative and ritual
skills weaken accordingly.
The Inspector is charged with ensuring that each Lodge leader is
provided sufficient instruction to ensure that he can perform both the RITUAL
and ADMINISTRATION duties to the highest degree possible. To make this
evaluation, the Inspector MUST be able to review the answers to the OfficerElect’s Questions for Advancement as well as observe the ritual work as
performed by the involved officer-elect prior to his being installed. In most
cases the “re-qualification” of Past Masters comes in the form of the Inspector’s
personal knowledge and RECENT observations of the Past Master’s abilities. In
other cases where the Inspector has not had the opportunity to observe the recent
activities of the Past Master, it must be obtained by having the Past Master
demonstrate his abilities. Only when the Inspector is satisfied that the Past
Master possesses appropriate administrative and ritual skills to perform the duty
of the station about to be filled will the Inspector consider the Past Master to be
qualified.
If the officer-elect has been a Past Master within the past six years and
still possesses satisfactory Administrative and Ritual skills, then he should not
be concerned at having to demonstrate the level of his proficiency before the
Inspector.
The person occupying the Oriental Chair possesses a most awesome
responsibility. He is responsible for ALL THE RITUAL WORK that is
performed in his Lodge. In order to accept that responsibility he MUST possess
the skills of both the Junior and Senior Warden, not to mention other officer
positions. To expect less creates situations where substandard administrative
and/or ritual work performed by subordinates may not be recognized by the
Master until after it is too late to make necessary corrections. Therefore, it is
vitally important that the Inspector is satisfied that the Brother who serves as
Master is sufficiently qualified in both the Junior and Senior Warden skills.
History has taught us that the best course of action is to maintain the
current practice of requiring officers to demonstrate to the Inspector their
CURRENT level of proficiency prior to being installed into their respective
positions. Those Past Masters who have remained current will easily qualify
and those who have not will be motivated to return to the highest degree of
proficiency. We therefore do not support this resolution.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Resolution No. 07-05
Conviction Of A Crime Of Moral Turpitude
Resolution WITHDRAWN.

Resolution No. 07-06
Early Presentation Of Golden Veteran Awards
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow presentation of Golden Veteran
Awards at any time commencing 6 months preceding the anniversary date.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the average age of our new membership is between 40-45
years, their age at time of eligibility for a Golden Veteran Award will be
between 90-95 years; and
WHEREAS, as our members age and their health diminishes,
Inspectors are often called upon to obtain permission to present a 50-year
Golden Veterans Award to a Brother who was close to, but had not yet reached,
the exact date of his 50th Anniversary; and
WHEREAS, in such cases, Inspectors are forced to wait to present
those pins until such time as the Brothers, God willing, lived to see their exact
anniversary dates. Unfortunately, in all too many cases, presentation of those
pins is suspended when our Brother is called to work in that Celestial Lodge
above and we were not allowed to appropriately honor him here on Earth; and
WHEREAS, Past Grand Masters have often wrestled with this
problem to hold fast to the “Masonic rule of law,” by holding their breaths in
hopes the Brother would remain healthy enough to receive his honor; and
WHEREAS, it is important to know exactly what makes one eligible
for just such an award. California Masonic Code Section 408.010, as well as the
Golden Veteran’s Award Ceremony, identified the key eligibility words as
“member,” “in good standing,” and the required number of “years;” and
WHEREAS, God gives each of us a different length of earthly cable
tow; each strong at the start and like a brittle thread near the end, we know not
how long our earthly cable tows are. As we age we can often tell when the cable
tow begins to become brittle. When that time arrives we and/or our family
begin to make plans for the future; plans that are not controlled by anyone but
the Supreme Architect of the Universe. These plans often involve the timely
reception of deserved honors. One such honor is the number of years a Brother
was a member in good standing within his Lodge; and

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

153

WHEREAS, when a Brother has found the TIME to support this
Fraternity by paying his dues for 50 years, we should NEVER be in a position of
wishing we had found the TIME to have presented him with his just rewards
prior to his death; and
WHEREAS, we should be able to honor a Brother for the support he
has provided to the Fraternity IN ADVANCE of his death; and
WHEREAS, during the 2006 Annual Communication of the Grand
Lodge, the members approved the early presentation of the 75-year pin, and a
clarification of this rule is also in order; and
WHEREAS, if passed, California will join the other 32 states in
America that allow such timely presentations.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 408.010 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§408.010. GOLDEN VETERAN AND DIAMOND JUBILEE AWARDS.
The Grand Lodge, upon request, shall provide a Golden Veteran’s Award to
members of this Jurisdiction who have been Master Masons in good standing for
a total of 50 years or more, presentation being made by the Grand Master,
Inspector or their representative with appropriate ceremony. The presentation of
the Golden Veteran Award may be made at any time commencing with the date
six months prior to the date on which the member shall otherwise become
eligible.
The Grand Lodge, upon request, shall provide a Diamond Jubilee Award to
members of this Jurisdiction who have been Master Masons in good standing for
a total of 75 years or more, presentation being made by the Grand Master or his
designee with appropriate ceremony. The presentation of the Diamond Jubilee
Award may be made at any time commencing with the first day of during the
calendar year in which the member shall otherwise become eligible.
In the case of a deceased Mason, if he was eligible for but had not received the
Award prior to his death, or in the case of an incompetent Mason, presentation
on request may be made to the family or heirs.
In computing the length of time requisite for issuance of either award, a period
of up to one year, during which the member held a Demit from any Lodge
recognized by this Grand Lodge, may be included; provided, however, periods
during which the member held a Demit for more than one year from the date of
the Demit, or was suspended for non-payment of dues, must be added to the
time when the member would otherwise have been eligible to receive the award.
The period between the issuance of a Demit for the purpose of becoming a
charter member of a new Lodge and the institution of the new Lodge is not an
interruption of the required number of years of continuous membership.

154

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
This proposed Resolution seeks to amend California Masonic code
408.010 regarding the time period for presentation of the Golden Veteran’s
Award by permitting its presentation at any time within six months of his
completion of fifty years as a member in good standing in Masonry. At any age,
fifty years of membership will no doubt find most men near the end of their
cable-tow, good health notwithstanding. This proposed resolution, as its title
implies, is being presented as a way of loosening the binds put upon Grand
Lodge that forces it to use an “exact time” requirement for presentation of the
award, which may cause many men in their senior years to miss out on this
auspicious occasion.
Any man with fifty years of Masonry has achieved a rare and special
place among Masons. This proposed Resolution provides nothing more than the
addition of a six-month “grace period” for presentation of the Golden Veteran’s
Award. Having made application to a Lodge and having been accepted to
receive the degrees in Masonry, these potential recipients have, in fact, been
Masons in their hearts more than fifty years.
Passage of this proposed resolution will take what might otherwise be
punitive wording in the California Masonic code, and allow it to be more “user
friendly” by giving the same grace period to Grand Lodge for its presentation to
this brother and his family.
This Resolution is consistent with the policies and general purpose of
this Grand Lodge.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 07-07
Foreign Degree Conferrals
The proponents of this Resolution seek to conform the Code to previously
unwritten procedures followed by custom authorizing foreign degree conferrals.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, our fraternal relations with other Grand Jurisdictions can
be greatly enhanced by exchanging degree conferrals; and
WHEREAS, many of us have enjoyed and learned a great deal when
we have witnessed a degree conferred in California by teams from other Grand

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

155

Jurisdictions, such as the Oklahoma Indian degree team conferral at the 2006
Annual Communication; and
WHEREAS, Masons in other Grand Jurisdictions should have similar
opportunities to witness the California work conferred in their Grand
Jurisdictions; and
WHEREAS, it is helpful to conform the Code to previously unwritten
procedures followed by custom regarding these foreign degree conferrals.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that Section 804.680 of
the California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§804.680. CONFERRAL OF DEGREES IN THIS JURISDICTION BY
ANOTHER JURISDICTION.
After consultation with the Grand Lecturer and the Committee on Ritual, the
Grand Master may permit a Lodge in this Jurisdiction to invite a Lodge or a
degree team from another Jurisdiction to confer a degree in this Jurisdiction the
inviting Lodge upon a candidate, Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft from the
inviting Lodge, or from the invited Jurisdiction, using the ritual of such other
Jurisdiction. Any such invitation must be in writing and transmitted by the
Grand Master or Grand Secretary of this Jurisdiction to the Grand Secretary of
the invited Jurisdiction. An elected candidate of a Lodge in this Jurisdiction
may receive the Entered Apprentice degree under this section and an Entered
Apprentice or Fellow Craft of a Lodge in this Jurisdiction may receive a degree
under this section as long as the requirements of Section 804.660 of this Code
are followed. A candidate from another Jurisdiction may receive a degree under
this section as long as the requirements of Section 804.670 of this Code are
followed.
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, that a new Section 804.685 be added
to the California Masonic Code to read as shown:

§804.685. CONFERRAL OF DEGREES IN ANOTHER JURISDICTION.
After consultation with the Grand Lecturer and the Committee on Ritual, the
Grand Master may permit a Lodge in this Jurisdiction to accept an invitation
from a Lodge in another Jurisdiction to confer a degree in the inviting Lodge
upon a candidate, Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft from the invited Lodge, or
from the inviting Jurisdiction, using the ritual of this Jurisdiction. Any such
invitation must be in writing and transmitted by the Grand Master or Grand
Secretary of the inviting Jurisdiction to the Grand Secretary of this Jurisdiction.
An elected candidate of a Lodge in this Jurisdiction may receive the Entered

156

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Apprentice degree under this section and an Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft
of a Lodge in this Jurisdiction may receive a degree under this section as long as
the requirements of Section 804.660 of this Code are followed.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The practice of the Grand Master to grant permission for degree work
from another Jurisdiction to be performed in this Jurisdiction has been in effect
for many, many years. The proponents of this Resolution seek to codify this
accepted procedure and custom in the conferral of foreign degrees within this
Grand Lodge and for our Ritual to be presented in another Jurisdiction by
adding new California Masonic Code section 804.865. Historically, the Grand
Master has granted such requests four to five times per year.
Although the Grand Master has the power to grant permission for these
degrees, and has done so for over 150 years, this practice is not specifically
permitted in the California Masonic Code. This Resolution corrects that
situation. This Resolution also contains language that clarifies the current
language of Section 804.680, and also codifies existing practice that the request
and permission process for permitting foreign degrees to be conducted in this
Jurisdiction be in writing.
This Resolution is consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Ritual:
Brethren of the Grand Lodge, Resolution No.2007-07 entitled “Foreign
Degree Conferrals” seeks to bring the Code into conformity with the current
unwritten procedures for authorizing foreign degree conferrals. It proposes to
change Section 804.680 of the Code so that it relates only to the Conferral of
Degrees in this Jurisdiction by another Jurisdiction by eliminating the sentence
relating to Section 804.660 regarding courtesy degrees for a California candidate
by another Jurisdiction. It also adds the requirement that an invitation from a
California Lodge to a Lodge or degree team in another Jurisdiction to conduct a
degree using the other Jurisdiction ritual must be in writing and transmitted by
the Grand Master or Grand Secretary of this Jurisdiction to the Grand Secretary
of the invited Jurisdiction.
In addition, a new Section 804.685 is created which covers the
Conferral of Degrees in another Jurisdiction using California ritual in response
to an invitation from a Lodge in the other Jurisdiction. Any such invitation must
be in writing and transmitted by the Grand Master of Grand Secretary of the
inviting Jurisdiction to the Grand Secretary of this Jurisdiction.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

157

The Ritual Committee is satisfied the proposed changes to Section
804.680 contain the necessary controls regarding the authorization of the
conferral of degrees in this Jurisdiction by another Jurisdiction utilizing the
Ritual fo the visiting Jurisdiction. The conferral of degrees in another
Jurisdiction utilizing the Ritual of California when invited to do so by the other
Jurisdiction are appropriately covered in the new Section 804.685.
We therefore support this resolution.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Board Meetings
Meetings of the full Board were held on the first Wednesday in the
months of July, September, November, January, April, and May, alternating
between the Union City and Covina campuses. In the months in which a Board
meeting was not scheduled, meetings of the key committees of the Board,

Development Committee and the Children’s Services/Scholarship Committee,
met at least quarterly or as needed. In addition the Board held its annual twoday strategic planning retreat in March, 2007 in Riverside.
Finance Committee
By Larry L. Adamson, Chairman
It has been an honor and privilege to serve as Chairman of the Masonic
Homes of California Finance Committee. This committee monitors and
manages the finances for the Masonic Homes of California and is charged with
the important responsibility of ensuring that we serve our residents today, while
protecting our legacy for future generations. These are solemn obligations,
which I and every member of my committee, take very seriously. The primary
role of the Finance Committee is to review and recommend approval to the
Board of Trustees of the operating and capital budgets and to monitor actual
performance against those approved budgets. Other important responsibilities of
this committee include:


Reviewing recommendations from the Investment Committee of Grand
Lodge regarding investment strategies for Homes’ funds as well as
their recommendations for spending guidelines that establish the
parameters for the operating and capital budgets.



Working with recommendations from the Insurance Committee of
Grand Lodge to establish financially sound risk management and
coverage structures.



Reviewing and approving the reports from external auditors as well as
special internal audits that are conducted as appropriate.



In collaboration with the Strategic Planning Committee, overseeing
long-term financial planning as well as the review of financial analysis
relating to new programs, operational initiatives and capital projects.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA


163

Advising the full Board as to the appropriate sources of funding for all
proposed projects and programs.

The Masonic Homes of California ended the fiscal 2007 with a
favorable budget variance of $1.175 million, of which $1.03 million was
favorable revenue variance due to pharmacy & resident asset & $145K was
variable expense variance due to tight expense management and lower than
anticipated expenses. These results were provided using the un-audited
financials for the Masonic Homes of CA.
For the past several years, operating expenditures of MHC have been
guided by spending parameters set as a percentage of a five-year rolling average
of the investment balances of the general and endowment funds. This approach
insures our operating budget will be somewhat buffered from significant yearto-year market swings while protecting the integrity of the fund balances for
future generations of residents. This committee is committed to constantly reevaluating the operations of the Homes to ensure we are operating efficiently, all
the while maintaining the highest level of care and resident satisfaction. In
addition, we continue to explore opportunities to diversify the revenue sources
of the Homes to better protect our assets for future generations.
The Finance Committee reviewed regular reports from the Grand
Treasurer. These reports include quarterly investment performance reports,
monthly operating statements, quarterly capital project reviews and various
others. Moss Adams, LLP the new auditor for Grand Lodge and the Masonic
Homes, completed the annual external audit for the fiscal year ended June 30,
2007 and will be reporting on their findings at a meeting scheduled for October.
The Finance Committee worked with management to develop the fiscal
year 2008 budget using a zero-based approach. In the face of ever rising costs,
management proposed, the Finance committee recommended and the Board
approved a budget which came in at 4.45%, below the 5% spending guideline.
The Finance Committee also worked with management to develop a
capital project budget to assure the Homes remain well-maintained and safe to
protect the comfort and safety of our residents. The Homes continues to fund the
Capital Reserve Fund from annual depreciation through the operating budget.
This fund is used to pay for capital projects and to build a reserve for future
needs. The budget developed for FY2008 will allow the balance in this fund to
grow for future capital needs.
During the course of this year, this Committee also oversaw the
development and implementation of several new and important initiatives and
accomplishments, which include:

164

PROCEEDINGS OF THE



In the course of preparing for construction of the Acacia Creek Senior
Living Communities, we obtained, for the first time ever, a credit rating for
the Masonic Homes of California. I am happy to announce that Fitch
Ratings Agency gave us a score of AA-, making us the highest rated senior
living community in the United States. Standard and Poors subsequently
gave us a AA rating, confirming our status and credit worthiness.



We extended a line of credit from our Strategic Reserve Fund to the
Acacia Creek Board of Directors with terms that call for a payback at the
time of their financing comparable to our average investment returns. We
also negotiated land leases for both campuses and the terms of a
management services agreement and a credit enhancement agreement.



We applied to become a certified provider with Medicare and MediCal
for our skilled nursing unit on our Union City campus. We will be limiting
admissions to this unit to residents of the Masonic Homes and the Acacia
Creek at Union City community. In so doing, we will generate new revenue
and allow our residents to access the benefits to which they are entitled.
We anticipate that we will be able to begin billing for this new revenue
shortly, which will significantly extend the reach of our charitable dollar.
We have contracted with an experienced billing agency to lead us through
this process.



Consistent with the senior living industry and the business community
at large, we froze the California Masonic Retirement 403(b) plan effective
March 31, 2007. All participants were deemed 100% vested upon
termination of that Plan. We adopted a new 401(k) plan including
providing an employer contribution effective April 1, 2007.



As a result of changes to the CMC adopted at the 2006 Annual
Communication, a separate audit committee of Grand Lodge was formed.
At the Finance Committee’s recommendation, the MHC Board delegated
the audit function to the GL audit committee.



Toward the end of the fiscal year, we authorized an emergency
$230,000 funding request for the Union City campus, over and above the
approved capital budget for FY 2007. This was necessary to replace all
HVAC electrical motors in our skilled nursing unit in response to an
emerging safety threat.



We approved the demolition and asbestos removal of the old Acacia
apartments on the Union City campus. These buildings represented a safety
hazard and were demolished this year.

The above represents just the highlights of this committee’s efforts this
year. I want to personally thank every member of this hard-working committee.

Without their commitment, their business acumen and their profound dedication
to our mission, we could not have achieved the above. I also want to thank the
full Board of Trustees, particularly its President, Allen B. Gresham, PGM for
entrusting these important responsibilities to our care and for their ongoing
support.

Strategic Planning Committee
By George L. Geanoulis
In November 2003, the Board of Trustees adopted a three-year strategic
plan for the Masonic Homes of California that guided and directed our efforts.
This year the Strategic Planning Committee assessed our progress on the goals
set forth at that time and established the vision for the organization for the next
three years. We identified three strategic imperatives that established the
foundation and context for all that we do:

166

PROCEEDINGS OF THE


Protecting and furthering the mission of the Masonic Homes of
California;



Protecting and optimizing the financial resources of the organization;
and,



Positioning the organization to respond to the changing and emerging
needs of the membership.

HOW AND WHAT WE KNOW ABOUT THE NEEDS OF OUR
FRATERNITY
We have conducted multiple focus groups, surveys and ongoing
research to learn about the needs and desires of our membership in order to
create programs and services that reflect the many and varied needs of our
fraternal family. Our research has demonstrated the following characteristics
about our fraternal family:


The majority of those over the age of 60 planned responsibly for
retirement yet many still find themselves without the resources
necessary to live out their lives with dignity and self sufficiency. The
financial resources that would have been sufficient to provide for them
into their 70’s can run out as they enter into their 80’s, 90’s and beyond
– just at the time that their need for assistance is greater than ever. To
further complicate the picture, stock market performance has been
volatile and many long-term investments have lost some or all of their
value.



Our elderly widows are the most vulnerable members of our fraternal
family. Nationally, elderly widows are three times more likely to live
in poverty and isolation than their male counterparts. Our own survey
showed that the widowed respondents were significantly less affluent
than their Masonic counterparts.



It is an unfortunate fact that the constituents most likely to need
services are also the most likely to be disconnected from the fraternity.
Many have not been able to attend lodge for years - in the meantime
their lodge may have consolidated- and as a result, they and/or their
widows can lose touch with their fraternal support system just at the
time they may need it most.



Approximately 90% of our current resident base would be unable to
access services in any other Continuing Care Retirement Community
given their relatively low income/asset base. Although we do offer Fee
For Service (FFS) entrance options, our primary constituents remain
the low/low-middle income elderly.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

167



In many communities in California, the middle-income seniors have
few choices regarding long-term care and are in need of affordable
services and programs.



Middle-aged Masons are members of the “sandwich generation”, which
means they are coping with age-related issues in their parents and lodge
brothers while raising their own families.

STRATEGIC PRIORITIES AND DIRECTION
In establishing our strategic priorities, we focused on ensuring our
services reflect the identified characteristics and needs of our fraternal family.
Our challenge is to address these needs while protecting and preserving our
charitable assets for future generations.
In the past few years, we have invested in the strategic growth of our
Masonic Outreach Services (MOS) program as an immediate way of serving our
most vulnerable members.
During the past three years, we’ve restructured our programs and
services to accomplish the following:


Developed a new centralized intake department to streamline and
improve the admission process for all of our communities



Created procedures for serving those in need of Assisted Living
through community partnerships



Created a new case management program for those needing help
accessing necessary services (without needing financial support)



Accessing all forms of available reimbursement to reduce the cost per
case



Increased emphasis on lodge and family involvement in the care and
support of our MOS clients

The MOS program grew by over 20% last year; such growth, while
exciting and necessary, makes our charitable dollar that much more important.

REVENUE PRODUCING STRATEGIES
We applied to become a certified provider with Medicare and MediCal
for our skilled nursing unit on our Union City campus. In so doing, we will
generate new revenue and allow our residents to access the benefits to which
they are entitled. We will start accessing this new revenue soon, which will
significantly extend the reach of our charitable dollar.

168

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

ACACIA CREEK COMMUNITIES
We have negotiated long-term land leases and a management services
agreement with Acacia Creek at Union City and Acacia Creek at Covina. We
are very pleased with the development of these communities under the
leadership of the Acacia Creek Boards of Directors. These new communities
will expand our services to our brethren and fill an unmet need for affordable
retirement services in California. They will also produce new revenue for the
Masonic Homes of California in support of our charitable mission.

CONCLUSION
Our commitment to our brethren is to create a long-term, sustainable
financial plan for the organization that ensures the continuity and perpetuity of
our historical mission. As Chairman, I would like to thank all the members of
this committee for their tireless efforts on behalf of our organization. By
working together, we are doing our part to preserve an historical legacy of
caring that extends for more than 109 years. It is an honor to be part of such a
service. I here acknowledge our members who have served so faithfully this
year:
Allan B. Gresham, PGM

Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California, Secretary

President’s Report
By Allen B. Gresham
It is a great pleasure and an honor to update our fraternity on the
wonderful work being conducted by the Masonic Homes of California. I believe
that the communities, the buildings and the care provided through the Masonic
Homes represent the best and highest of our Craft – our shared commitment to
Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth.
I, and every Trustee on our Board, share this pride and are committed
to upholding the great legacy established by our forward-thinking ancestors.
Our programs and services have changed and evolved over our history
in response to the changing needs of our fraternity and the changing face of our
society. They’ve had to. What has not – and never will – change is our
commitment to caring for our brethren and their families in the name, and on the
behalf, of every Mason in California.
What does this mean? I think a story will best illustrate exactly what
this means. One of our brethren, at the age of 87, passed away leaving his 84
year old widow behind. Upon his death, the widow found that his long illness
had used up most of their savings and that the pension that was her primary
financial support stopped on his death. Her husband had always told her that if
she ever needed help to ask his Lodge. So she did. The lodge secretary reassured
her and provided her information about our Masonic Outreach Services, or MOS
program as it is known. In less than a month, the care team of the MOS
program had developed a short-term and long-term plan for this widow and had
started providing her ongoing financial support. Her care manager visits and
calls her regularly, while lodge members continue to invite her to lodge events.
When told of the help and care she would receive, she started to cry and
said, “He’s still taking care of me”
This is what it means when we say we provide care in the name and on
the behalf of every Mason in California. And this is what is possible through the
generosity of generations of Masons who have given a donation to the Annual
Fund directed to the MOS program. The dollars that are given today are put to
use for such a widow tomorrow.
Overall it’s been an exciting year at the Masonic Homes of California.
In our residential program at the Masonic Homes, over 500 seniors
have found a home in our Covina and Union City communities. And in this
home, they are being provided care in a manner in which we can all take pride.
In fact, our resident satisfaction scores place us among the top 2% of retirement
providers in the country.

170

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In addition to providing financial support to members in need, the MOS
program is now providing case management services. These services are
designed to meet the needs of those who don’t require financial support but do
need help navigating through the range of services and programs available to
seniors. The MOS program is larger – 20% larger – than ever before in its
history and is helping each one of us fulfill our Masonic obligations to our
brothers and their widows.
Thirty-six children between the ages of 4 and 17 currently live in our
residential program on the Covina campus. We are following the “Circle of
Courage” program model and are seeing exciting improvements in children’s
grade point averages and their overall well-being. We are serving an additional
60 children and families through our Family Resource Center. This is a service
designed to help children and families in need gain access to the supportive
services and resources available to them.
On a more somber note, I must update the Craft on the status of
ongoing litigation regarding our children’s program. On July 24, 2007, a jury in
Pomona, California awarded a total of $12 million to three men who claimed
they were sexually molested between 1974 and 1979 by a former staff member
while they were children living at Masonic Homes. This former staff member
adamantly denies the three men’s accusations of abuse.
The three men first revealed the existence of these claims in 2003,
almost 30 years after the alleged sexual abuse. By that time, a number of key
witnesses important to a fair decision in this case had died or were incapacitated
due to illness Because of the total absence of defense witnesses and the lack of
documentary evidence, our attorney’s were unable to effectively rebut the
plaintiff’s allegations. Our attorneys will be appealing the decision in this case
as a result of these and other limitations. We expect to be involved in ongoing
litigation for the next year on this and other open cases.
The Masonic Home for Children in Covina has been in operation for
more than 100 years. During all that time, it has been dedicated to providing the
best quality of care to children who reside there. We remain dedicated to this
goal today.
It is important to emphasize that none of the individuals identified as
alleged or potential perpetrators of abuse in the cases are still employed by, or
reside in the Masonic Homes. Additionally, California regulations for
residential childcare facilities have become more strenuous since the 1960’s and
our most recent survey from the State confirms that we are meeting or exceeding
these strict standards. When we couple these standards from the State with our
own policies and procedures, we are certain that the residents in our programs
today are safe and receiving high quality care.
And now to the future. Two years ago at the Annual Communication,
we held focus group sessions to determine the membership’s interest in building

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

171

new Masonic Senior Living Communities. Our surveys and focus groups told
us that indeed the fraternity was interested and accordingly, we formed two new
corporations: Acacia Creek at Covina and Acacia Creek at Union City. Each of
these corporations has their own Board of Directors which has been charged
with the responsibility of developing and marketing these communities.
The MHC Board of Trustees has been following their progress very
closely. And it is with great pride and excitement that I here report that the
dream of Acacia Creek at Covina and Acacia Creek at Union City is getting
close to reality. We told you last year that if they came – we would build. They
have come and the Acacia Creek Boards are hoping to begin construction in
early 2008. In fact, on Sunday, October 7th at 10:00am there will be a
groundbreaking ceremony for Acacia Creek at Union City on the Union City
campus.
In closing, I want to thank you all for your support of the grand mission
of the Masonic Homes. It is an honor and a privilege to be a member of this
Board. It is a tremendous responsibility and one that I, and every Trustee, take
very seriously. I here want to thank each and every member of the Board of
Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California for the great efforts and tireless
dedication to this great institution. I can say that in all that we do, we are guided
by our commitment to serving our brethren in a manner reflective of our
fraternal ideals.
Respectfully submitted,
Allen B. Gresham, PGM, President

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
MASONIC HOME AT UNION C ITY
As I enter into my seventh year as the Executive Director and
Administrator of the Masonic Home, I am honored to represent the residents of
this fine community and look forward to the events that are ahead of us in the
coming year.
Over these past two years we saw a shift in the acuity status of our
residents. By admitting both assisted living and independent residents directly
into the home, we discovered a shift of acuity on campus with an increase in the
percentage of assisted living residents. Since our assisted living apartments are
limited, the demand for those apartments has increased, due to assisted living
admissions into the Home as well as existing independent residents who need
assisted living for their health care needs. To correct this shift, we have needed
to limit the amount of assisted living residents admitted directly into the Home.
The Admission Committee acted quickly by implementing a directive that only
admitted independent residents. This change in the admission process has
reverted the census back to the original ratio for each level of care.
Last year we had completed a new main entrance to the Home. We
widened the two lane entrance to three lanes at the new guard station area. The
new guard shack is located in the center of the road and on a concrete island.
There is an electronic wrought iron gate requiring a proximity card for entry
after hours. Beginning with the 2007 fiscal year, a security officer will be
stationed at the gate twelve hours per day instead of 24 hours. Security cameras
are now located at the Communications desk which monitors the gate after
hours. Installation of the electronic gate allowed us to reduce our security
service fee by approximately fifty percent which amounted to an annual
reduction in our security service’s budget of approximately $70,000.
One of our most exciting additions to the Masonic Home is the creation
of Siminoff Lodge UD, the first Masonic Lodge at the Masonic Home in Union
City established by and for the residents of the Homes. In June of 2006, the
Siminoff Masonic Club was established which became the Siminoff Masonic
Lodge UD in September 2006. The idea of the Lodge began under the
leadership, motivation and guidance of Virgil Land, who is the coach of the
Lodge. Siminoff Lodge UD consists of fifty Master Masons. As a result of
many hours of practice devoted to the ritual and competency of the Team, the
local inspector has notified the Grand Lodge that the Degree Team is now
certified to confer all three Masonic degrees in their entirety. It is expected that
Siminoff Masonic Lodge will obtain its constitution in October of 2007.
Siminoff Masonic Lodge UD consists of the following Master Masons:
Rosser “Doc” Adamson, Alex Adorador, Sterling Bailey, John Bull, Mort
Collis, Allan Davidson, Irwin Fershleiser, Jim Gloeckler, Harold Harrison,
Forest “Woody” Hibbs, Bob Huntington, Virgil Land, Ralph Loomis, Bob

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

173

McCain, Jack McClellan, Ray Rosson, Leonard Smith, Jack Tomlinson and
George Turegano, Andrew Phipps, William Fretz, Arbie Pierson, Bill Teuteberg,
Keith Wong, Wally Hegland, Jack Goodwin, Bill McNerney, Fred Clodfelter,
George Dolfin, Mike Kilbury, Emmett Nail, Richard Kirkman, Stephen Gassett,
Chuck Silverman and Mitch Franceshini.
Another highlight on campus is the new Masonic Garden Club. We
have converted the Second Adams lawn area into a large community garden and
there are currently over fifteen residents who are participating in this group
which is overseen by our Director of Dining Services. At this time we have
installed nine raised planter beds, twenty two rows of vegetables consisting of
thirty varieties of veggies. The residents have planted over two hundred
tomatoes and over three hundred corn plants along with many other vegetables.
The Garden Club also oversees a large green house for plant seedlings started
during the winter months. This garden supplies our kitchen with many fresh fruit
and vegetables and a variety of herbs. The Masonic Garden Club has converted
 acre of additional gardens in the Spring of 2007.
In 2006 our residents raised over $2,000 from several white elephants
sales and all proceeds were directed to the City of Union City where several
families in need of financial assistance received donations during this past
holiday season.
Each December we welcome over 1,500 visitors to our Home to be
entertained by the local Elementary, Middle and High School students signing
holiday carols. The community and staff filled the Toys for Tots sleigh which
was overflowing with toys. Santa made an appearance and assisted our 101 year
resident, Mrs. Velma Pflug, to turn on our 108 foot Holiday tree.
Over 153 volunteers came to our campus and decorated eighteen
Christmas trees. This year more of our Masonic Fraternal families participated
in our holiday events. Paying special attention to our Skilled Nursing residents,
the volunteers asked each resident for a short wish list and on Christmas day
each one of our sixty-eight residents had a personalized stocking filled with their
wish list items.
Notable resident achievements this year included the birthdays of

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

183

REPORT OF THE TRUSTEES OF THE
CALIFORNIA MASONIC FOUNDATION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Foundation Trustees are pleased to report that our initiatives to
further strengthen our support of youth in California continue strong. Our
scholarship awards to deserving college students garner many more requests
than we can fill. To train more teachers in helping our youth, our MSAP
program is being expanded. The Foundation continues building a strong
relationship with the California Department of Education to better serve the
youth of our jurisdiction.

Fundraising Efforts
My Brethren, fundraising, including individual, lodge, and community
support is going to be a key element of our success in continuing to support
valuable programs in this jurisdiction.
I am pleased to report that this year the California Masonic Foundation
raised the most annual funds in its 38 year history, with over $500,000 received.
Masonic Education and Leadership Training, the Grand Master's Project, and
the Endomosaic Mural project all were areas of increased financial support.

College Scholarship Program
Our scholarship program remains a cornerstone of our Craft’s
commitment to youth. On behalf of all California masons, the Foundation
awarded 152 general scholarships, totaling $904,000. Of these 57 were new,
and 95 were renewals from prior years.
The C.E. Towne Scholarship, named for Brother Charles E. Towne,
accounted for 47 scholarships this year, with 39 of them renewals, and eight
new.
Each Towne Scholarship is approximately a $40,000, four-year
commitment. Including both new and renewing students, the C.E. Towne
Scholarship distribution totaled $710,000 in 2007.
Students receiving Masonic scholarships this year all matriculated to
excellent schools, including more than ten to the Ivy leagues, and more than 30
to top California schools including Stanford, Berkeley, and UCLA. We are
proud to be supporting these amazing young people, and hope you share our
pride at being able to help them meet the high costs of their educations.
We are also proud to say that every one of our scholarship awards went
to a young man or woman who had a Masonic relationship of some sort, either
as a son or daughter, or a participant in our youth orders. We encourage

184

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

members of our youth orders to apply, as well as sons, daughters, grandsons,
and granddaughters of Masons. A Masonic affiliation, a solid grade point, and
financial need are the basic criteria we use to determine who receives this
support. Please join me in congratulating these fine young men and women.

Masonic Student Assistance Program
The Masonic Student Assistance Program (MSAP) continues to
provide a much-needed service to our public schools, funded by the generosity
of the Craft. This year we will have trained more teachers than in any year since
2000, with nearly 450 teachers and school personnel benefiting from our
support. As our program has grown in popularity, we have seen an opportunity
to deliver a critically important service directly to our communities.
Also this past year we changed our method of delivery to a regional
training model, which takes MSAP directly to several communities across the
state. It is our hope to be providing 10 regional MSAP trainings per year within
three years, and to train more than 1,000 teachers each year. Our commitment is
to become the clear leader in supporting student assistance programs statewide
in general, and our Masonic Model program in particular. We are well on our
way to doing just that.
We are also pleased to be partnering with local lodges, like Vesper #84
in Red Bluff, which has made a significant charitable gift to assist bringing
MSAP to Tehama and Butte counties. Please join me in recognizing the
brethren of Vesper #84 in Red Bluff. Brethren: Thank you for supporting our
“gift” of this valuable training to our heroes in the classroom.
Programs such as MSAP are critically important to our communities
and neighborhoods. By helping teachers, administrators, and other school
personnel develop strategies for dealing with, and intervening on behalf of,
children who are at-risk, we do our part to keep families strong, children
successful, and communities safe.

Child ID Program
Our Child ID program continues as the premier source of providing
parents with an invaluable record of their children to be used in time of
emergency. Our Child ID Committee works creditably up and down our state to
ensure that Lodges have the equipment and supplies to provide this service in
their local community. The committee chair, Worshipful Ronald Lee, Vicechair Worshipful “Red” Morse and all committee members are to be
commended for their hard work.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

Leadership Training for Youth Groups
Your Foundation continues to provide at each Annual Communication,
grants to our youth groups to underwrite leadership training for our leaders of
tomorrow. I am pleased to report that the Board this year increased the annual
amount per group to $2,000 from $1,500.

Masonic Education and Leadership Training
Your Foundation continues to support the training of our Lodge leaders
by underwriting the Wardens Leadership Retreats. These weekend retreats
provide our future Masters, and their ladies with valuable insight into Lodge
programs, officer corps asset strength, and ongoing community service projects.
The facilitator staff, comprised of the Leadership Development Committee and
other key individuals, volunteer their time over four weekend retreat sessions, to
provide experience, guidance, and encouragement to our wardens. It should be
noted that this training is of the highest caliber, and provided at minimal cost.

Cornerstone Grants
By continuing to grant $1000 to each public school that sponsors a
cornerstone ceremony, we build ties between local lodges and their community
schools.

California Department of Education
The relationship between the Foundation, and therefore all California
Masons, and the California Department of Education has grown exponentially
during the past year. Reporting upward to Governor Schwarzenegger, The
Governor’s Prevention Advisory Council High Rate Underage Users (HRUU)
workgroup was designed to address the intervention of high rate users of alcohol
among our youth, via student assistance programs. This HRUU workgroup
includes representatives from the California Department of Education and
several other state agencies, California Parent Teacher Association, and staff
representatives from the California Masonic Foundation.
To assist the HRUU workgroup in supplying Student Assistance
Program information kits to all school districts in the state, the Foundation
underwrote the printing costs. These kits explain all the various programs
available to assist our youth, including MSAP.
And to further illustrate our evolving relationship with the California
Department of Education, we are collaborating to hold the first California
statewide Student Assistance Program Conference during February, 2008. This
conference is aimed at school administrators, counselors, SAP coordinators,

nurses, and others involved in safe schools programs. It will be held in the

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF TRUSTEES
OF THE CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial Temple are pleased
to present the 52nd annual report to the Grand Lodge on issues concerning the
condition and management of the physical facility as well as the financial
condition of our Memorial Temple. This report is an overview and abstract of
activities this year. Those wanting or needing more detailed information are
referred to the minutes of the quarterly meetings of the Board of Trustees filed
in the Grand Lodge office. Full and complete audited financials are not included
in this report. Those wishing to may review these records which are included in
the appendices of these proceedings.
Our five year plan has been updated and revised to accommodate
additional work that needs to be done on the building, specifically the update
and repair of the HVAC system (Heat Ventilating Air Conditioning). At the
recommendation of Terry Hobson, Director of Operations, your board was
presented with a proposal to accomplish this work. This project will be spread
over a four year period at an approximate cost of $480,000. A final decision
should be made in October. During the year the emergency generator was
replaced at a cost of $85,000. The system was nearly 50 years old and needed to
be replaced.
This year marked the beginning of a lease agreement with the newly
formed Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc. (NHMC). This body will be responsible
for the operation of the building and the CMMT will continue to be the landlord
and receive rental income. Reasons for this new arrangement are discussed in
last year’s report as well as this year’s report from NHMC.
This year the CMMT and NHMC formed a “Task Force” to investigate
alternative marketing strategies for our building. Conversations are underway
with two companies who have expressed an interest in our theater. We have had
discussions with the hotels on Nob Hill about a cooperative effort to bring more
business to the “Hill.” In additional a new web site has been created.
Financially, the building has done well this year. We had 166 event
days and a total attendance of 171,989 for the fiscal year ending June 30, 2007.
Activities using the Auditorium, Exhibition Hall, California Rooms and a
combination count as event days.
Douglas Ismail, Director of Masonic Philanthropy, reports that we are
close to raising the necessary $400,000 to begin work on the restoration of the
endomosaic mural in the lobby. We believe this work will be done well in
advance of the rededication celebration scheduled for September 2008. Thank
you, Doug, for your hard work.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

189

The Henry Wilson Coil Library is open and our museum curator, Adam
Kendall, is regularly displaying items of serious interest to Masons as well as the
general public. All of this is located on the mezzanine.
Our Chief Operating Officer, Allan L. Casalou, continues to perform
with outstanding efficiency and creativeness. His lead in discussions with
parties interested in our theater has been exceptional and both boards are aware
of and appreciate his significant contributions.
Our Grand Secretary, John L. Cooper III, continues to provide serious
advice and counsel on many areas. His support and help are deeply appreciated.
On a personal note, I wish to thank our Grand Master, Melvyn B. Stein,
for his confidence in my appointment to this board and also for the active and
interested brethren who have been a part of a very interesting year. We are
entering a new era and appreciate the overall support of our executive
committee.

Ronald M. Charles

Respectfully submitted
David D. Johnson, President

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF DIRECTORS
OF THE NOB HILL MASONIC CENTER, INC.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
You will recall from the 2006 report of the Board of Trustees of the
California Masonic Memorial Temple, that on advice of the accounting firm
performing the annual audit as well as advice from tax attorneys, the Board of
the Masonic Memorial Temple authorized the re-establishment of a for profit
corporation called Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc. That decision was made to
comply with IRS rules and to preserve the non-profit status of our other entities.
Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc. has been formed and oversees the
operation and maintenance of the Nob Hill Masonic Center within the California
Masonic Memorial Temple including the auditorium, exhibit hall and the public
parking garage. This is done pursuant to a lease agreement with the owner,
California Masonic Memorial Temple, that was executed on February 16, 2007.
Nob Hill pays rent to the owner – currently 12% of revenues; employs
the staff who operate the building; pays property taxes and insurance as well as
utilities and maintenance.
During this year the staff has aggressively promoted the use of the
auditorium and exhibit hall by outside entities for concerts, lectures, corporate
meetings, trade shows, and notably the Department of Homeland Security of our
Federal Government for Naturalization Ceremonies.
We have come to realize however that it is difficult to promote the use
of our facility for individual performances which are booked by large firms who
operate concert halls across the country and book performances in their halls. In
view of this we are having discussions with the nation’s largest such firm. If
these discussions materialize, they would assume the responsibility of operating
the auditorium and exhibit hall under a lease agreement, with Nob Hill
continuing operation of the public parking garage. This could result in a
substantial increase in net income to the benefit of the CMMT and the Grand
Lodge.
An audited financial statement covering the fiscal year ending October
31, 2007 will be made a part of the annual Proceedings of this Grand Lodge;
however the following is a summary of actual income and expenses through July
2007 and projected income and expenses through October 2007 – the end of the
fiscal year.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE CALIFORNIA FREEMASON
MAGAZINE EDITOR-IN-CHIEF
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The intended purpose of the California Freemason is to inspire and
educate our members about the significance of Freemasonry. It is an important
resource in advancing the strategic objectives of the Grand Lodge and it
continues to serve as the singular communication from the Grand Lodge to all of
its members.
In 53 years, the magazine has had only three editors, each of whom
worked with an editorial board consisting of the Grand Master, the Grand
Secretary and the editor, as outlined in the Code. Through the years a number of
important milestones were achieved in the evolution of the publication. In 2002,
the decision was made to build the editorial content of each issue around a single
theme. The goal is to impress upon the readers important aspects of Masonry in
a focused manner. Themes over the last five years have included:
Freemasonry and the Idea of Liberty
Relief in the 21st Century
Freemasons and Music
Masonic Support of Education
Freemasonry and Hollywood
Freemasons and Their Art
The Life Long Process of Masonic Formation
Exploring Masonry and the Outdoors
Other themes have been historical in nature including an issue focused
on Freemasonry and the Western Frontier. The latest issue, published this
month, focuses on how Masons are making a difference. Each issue is divided
into different sections that focus the theme in a particular way. The sections
include In California, Lodge Spotlight, Masonic Education, History, Around the
World, the Masonic Homes and the Feature Story. Though the sections vary
somewhat from issue to issue, most every issue contains the Lodge Spotlight
section which features great work that the members are doing as a lodge or as
individuals.
Since 2004, the California Freemason has won top awards by the
Society of National Association Publications (SNAP) for content, design and
photography.
SNAP is an association of more than 750 membership
organizations in the country including civic organizations like Rotary
International, trade associations like the American Bar Association, charities
like the American Heart Association, and university alumni organizations. The

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

193

issue of the California Freemason on Freemasonry and the Western Frontier
received the silver award for the Best Single Topic Issue – recognizing it as the
second best among all of these organizations.
The editorial board realizes that, while outside recognition is important
and something we can be proud of, the recognition from our own members is
most important.
Two years after the editorial and design changes were implemented, a
reader survey was included in an issue of the magazine. Nearly 2,000 responses
were received.
When asked about their impression of editorial changes in the
magazine, 80% of the respondents said that the changes were an improvement,
with 50% saying that it was a vast improvement.
When asked about their impression of the art and photography changes
in the magazine, 89% of the respondents said that the changes were an
improvement, with 69% saying that it was a vast improvement.
Interestingly, 56% said they spend at least 30 minutes reading the
magazine, which is considered very high in the industry.
The editorial board strives to meet these expectations with each issue.
Over the last five years, we have sought to include stories that incite members to
think more deeply about Masonry. We have profiled many masons who have
done extraordinary deeds. In addition there have been dozens of articles about
significant achievements and successful programs by our lodges.
Before 2002, most issues were 16 pages. Today each issue is 24 pages
and we are looking for ways to increase page count even more. You may have
noticed in the Per Capita proposal for 2008, the budget is being increased to
publish the magazine bi-monthly instead of on a quarterly basis.
Respectfully submitted,
Allan L. Casalou, Editor in Chief

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON INSTITUTE FOR M ASONIC STUDIES

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
This is a report providing your Board with some information as to the
progress of the Board of Directors for the Institute of Masonic Studies for the
year 2006-2007. As this is the first year for this Board, much discussion was
had as to the direction for the Institute for the year. Studies were undertaken to
establish By-Laws which are compatible with those of the Foundation and the
Grand Lodge of California. To date the By-laws are not completed but hope to
have them accomplished by our meeting in November, 2007.

198

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The major task of the Institute of Masonic Studies for this Masonic
Year was the implementation of the Masonic Formation Task Force with the
Task Force in the field to assist the lodges in the implementation of the program.
Numerous sessions were held around the Jurisdiction with members becoming
certified to assist in this educational program. The Institute has authorized the
creation of a "take home" DVD on Freemasonry which may be given to the
Entered Apprentice Mason on the night of his initiation for showing to his
family. We hope this will be available during the 2007 Annual Communication.
Also, the Board of Directors of the Institute for Masonic Studies
became very involved with acquiring several Masonic Scholars and/or speakers
to provide various events throughout the year within the Jurisdiction. These
have included Eric Palazzo; Chris Hodapp (the author of the book Masonry for
Dummies), along with several exhibits held in the Henry Wilson Coil Library
and Museum. One exhibit which should prove to be interesting is the "Initiated
Eye" Exhibit which will be held in the California Room of the California
Masonic Memorial Temple August 15 through October 7, 2007. Another
exhibit which proved to be successful was entitled "Sacking the Temple". We
are anticipating having more exhibits such as these in the California Masonic
Memorial Building in the future.
The Institute also provides the direction and implementation for the
Annual California Masonic Symposium. This Masonic Year, it was held in San
Diego with the theme "Freemasonry in Mexico and Latin America". The San
Diego Valley of the Scottish Rite hosted the meeting along with the dinner. A
date for next years' Symposium will be held on August 22-23, 2008 in San
Francisco at the CMMT. The theme for this event has not yet been decided.
The Board of Directors will determine if holding more than one symposium
during the year is feasible, this might entail a "road show", which might entail
taking the same program to different parts of the Jurisdiction.
All in all, the Board of Directors is greatly appreciated to the California
Masonic Foundation for its leadership in the quest for more quality Masonic
Education in our State. It is our hope that the path the Board of Directors of the
Institute for Masonic Studies has taken assists in seeing this goal accomplished.

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON JURISPRUDENCE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
The Committee on Jurisprudence serves as the legal adviser to the
Grand Master and the legal adviser to the Grand Lodge when in session. The
Committee reviews proposed resolutions and recommendations to be considered
at the next Annual Communication and prepares any necessary legislation to
carry out the intent of the proponents. At the Annual Communication, it
comments on the legality of the Grand Master’s decisions and dispensations and
on the legal effect of the proposed recommendations and resolutions. After the
Annual Communication, it prepares the changes to the California Masonic Code
to reflect the legislation which was adopted.
In the Committee’s view, those of Grand Master Stein’s decisions and
dispensations coming to its attention were legal and within his discretion. In the
Committee’s view, the legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July 27th of
this year is sufficient to carry out the intent of the proponents, and the legislation
contained in Grand Master Stein’s recommendations which he will present in his
message is sufficient to carry out his intent. We believe that each piece of this
legislation has the legal effect set forth in it.
The legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July 27th of this year
and the legislation contained in Grand Master Stein’s recommendations require
no separate motion because they are automatically before this Annual
Communication. The presiding officer will announce the vote required for the
passage of each item before discussion begins. Any legislation which does not
amend the California Masonic Code or the Ritual requires a simple majority
only for adoption. Any amendment to the California Masonic Code or the
Ritual must be adopted by a five-sixths affirmative vote. If the amendment
receives a simple majority but less than a five-sixths affirmative vote, it carries
over to the next Annual Communication when only a two-thirds affirmative vote
is required for adoption.
We thank the Grand Master for the opportunity which he has given us
to serve this year.

David M. Ferreria
Jack R. Levitt, PGM
Paul D. Rosenberger

Respectfully submitted,
R. Stephen Doan, P.G.M., Chairman

John R. Heisner
Lester W. Miller
Richard L. Riemer, Consultant

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON LEADERSHIP DEVELOPMENT

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
Leadership Development is one of the key priorities outlined in the
Grand Lodge Strategic Plan released earlier this year. The Leadership
Development Committee has taken aggressive and dedicated steps to align our
efforts with this strategic plan.
Over 900 people participated in the leadership training programs
offered by the Grand Lodge in this fiscal year. The training programs include
the Wardens’ Leadership Retreats, Lodge Management Certification Programs,
and Inspectors’ Seminars.

Wardens’ Leadership Retreats
The focus of the Wardens’ Leadership Retreats is to prepare lodge
wardens to be leaders of Masonry in their community. Through a two-part
strategic planning program divided between the Junior and Senior Warden
Retreats, wardens learn to develop a vision for their lodge, goals and objectives
to meet that vision, and a financial plan to support the strategic initiatives of the
lodge. Time is also devoted to sharing ideas about lodge programming in the
areas of membership development, Masonic education, and community
involvement. The wardens are encouraged to bring their spouses and a special
training program is provided for them, in addition to their participation with the
wardens on certain subjects.

Lodge Management Certification Program
The Lodge Management Certification Program offers in-depth training
on some of the most fundamental lodge operations. The program covers five
subjects: Lodge Finance, Program Planning, Hall Association Management,
Membership Development, and Lodge Administration. Each subject has four
courses and the training takes one entire day. After completing that four course
in one of the above subjects, attendees are given an exam on the material. Those
who pass the exam are given a certificate in that subject. The Lodge Finance
module of the certification program was made available as an on-line course this
year. The Hall Association module will be the next course available on-line
early spring 2008.

201

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

Inspectors’ Seminars
These seminars include a four hour course for new Inspectors on the
eve of a full day of training for all Inspectors. The program includes training
and discussion on the overall leadership training strategy of the Grand Lodge,
technological resources, lodge administration and finance. Inspectors consider
ideas on how to be most effective in their leadership role.

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MEMBERSHIP DEVELOPMENT

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
My name is Gary L. Peterson, and I am a Past Master of Saddleback
Laguna Lodge No 672, and Chairman of the Membership Development
Committee.
It is my pleasure to present to you the Annual Report of the
Membership Development Committee for the 2007 Masonic year.
I would first like to acknowledge and thank the committeemen and
Grand Lodge staff members who throughout this year have continued to
contribute their time and energy in supporting the development of this
Committees’ successful programs. Those committee members are Wor. Gary L.
Peterson, Past Master of Saddleback Laguna Lodge No 672; Wor. James E.
Banta, Past Master of Fresno Lodge No 247; Wor. Salvatore A. Sciortino, Past
Master of Wisdom Lodge No 202; Wor Anil Comelo , Past Master of Navel
Lodge No 87; Stephen Hanover, Past Master of Trinity Lodge No 27; Wor Rick
Keltner, Past Master of Union Kit Carson Lodge No 58; Wor Frederick Votto,
Past Master of Liberal Arts Lodge No 677; and Brother Erik Borg, a member of
Saddleback-Laguna No 682.
The Grand Lodge staff members are Wor. Allan L. Casalou, Assistant
Grand Secretary and a Past Master of Acalanes Fellowship Lodge No 480 and
Jeff Reyes, Administrative Assistant.
Again this year the “Pass It On” program continued its new member
pattern of increasing numbers through the comprehensive program.
Presentations of the program were made at the Lodge Management Certification
events, as well as through the use of MDC booths at Grand Lodge Annual
Communication and Wardens Retreats.
I am pleased to report to you that we again set a record for new
membership within our Jurisdiction. The year end shows 1,226 new men raised
as Master Masons with many lodges raising over our goal of 5 per lodge.
Through the use of Grand Lodge’s membership programs, we believe it reflects
a growth in new Masons that should continue.
The new “On The Level” Masonic information program has also
completed its distribution this year, and we feel that this new tool will greatly
enhance a Lodges ability to reach out to the community in a clear and concise
manner when telling them about our fraternity. “On The Level” contains CD’s,
instructional manuals, marketing samples, and scripts that go along with the
informative power point presentation.
The Committee presented a Certificate of Excellence to each lodge that
attained the “Pass It On” goal of five new Master Masons raised during the year.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

203

In addition a traveling plaque was presented to the lodge which raised the most
Master Masons.
At the request of the Executive Committee the MDC began examining
the membership retention issue. After analyzing the issue, the MDC made a
number of recommendations contained within a new program under the working
title of “Engage”. The program of member engagement and retention, as
authorized by the Executive Committee, will contain, among many
comprehensive items things such as 1) a new member handbook, 2) Membersonly web site, 3) New family orientation, 4) other resources and tools for lodge
use. It is expected that through the use of these new tools and resources, the
overall retention figures should increase.
The MDC will continue to evaluate its programs as to overall
effectiveness of the individual components. Necessary adjustments will
continue to be made as the program implementation continues.
On behalf of the Membership Development Committee for the Grand
Lodge of the State of California, I wish to thank you and the Most Worshipful
Melvyn B. Stein for giving us the opportunity to update you on the progress and
activities of our committee.
Thank you.

Anil Comelo
Rick Keltner
Stephen Hanover

Respectfully Submitted,
Gary L. Peterson, Chairman

James E. Banta
Salvatore A Sciortino
Frederick Votto

204

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE ON
TRIAL REVIEW
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Committee on Trial Review has to date 38 cases in its inventory, of
which 30 have been resolved. Additionally, we had received eight applications
for restoration.
The committee has reviewed each of the trials numbered 2464, 2507,
2508, 2509, 2510, 2511, 2512, 2513, 2514, 2515, 2516, 2517, 2518, 2519, 2520,
2521, 2524, 2529, 2534 and 2535, and finds that in each case there was
sufficient compliance with the procedural requirements of our Constitution and
Ordinances; that the findings and conclusions are supported by the trial record;
and that there was no miscarriage of justice.
Your committee therefore submits and moves for adoption the
following resolution:
“Resolved that in each of the trials numbered above the judgment and
penalty imposed be and are ratified, affirmed and approved.”
Trial Record 2522
Your committee feels that this case requires special consideration in
that the member’s conduct had, over several years, become increasingly
aggressive, threatening, and disruptive. Even a direct, written order from the
Grand Master was ignored by this member. The committee recommends that
the penalty be increased to expulsion, and therefore submits and moves for
adoption the following resolution:
“Resolved that in trial number 2522, the penalty be expulsion.”
Expulsions by the Grand Master
(CMC Section 1404.820)
There were nine cases, numbered 2523, 2525, 2526, 2527, 2528, 2530,
2531, 2532 and 2533 in which the members were expelled without trial by the
Grand Master, pursuant to CMC Section 1404.820. Although each case was
examined to assure compliance with the Code, they were not subject to review
by the committee.
The committee had received applications for restoration in Trial
Records 2496, 2497, 2498, 2499 and 2500 but were not reviewed as they did not
comply with the CMC. In each case they had failed to apply to the lodge which
had expelled them.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

205

The Jurisprudence Committee has interpreted CMC Section 1404.820
as applying retroactively to any member who was convicted, in a state or federal
court, of an offense of moral turpitude, and as such, the applicants in Trial
Records 2477, 2480 and 2527 are ineligible to apply for restoration pursuant to
CMC Section 1404.1050. Their applications, therefore, were not reviewed by
this committee.
The committee would like to acknowledge the generous support and
cooperation of the Very Worshipful John L. Cooper, Pamela Lindsten and
Worshipful Albert Donnici.

Jack H. Chammas
David D. Goble
Thomas C. Letcher

Respectfully submitted,
Richard A. Meyers, Chairman

Donald R. Fretz, Consultant

Donald B. McClary
Maxwell S. Roth
Robert E. Scott
